Dimension identities, almost self-conjugate partitions, and BGG complexes for Hermitian symmetric pairs
William Q. Erickson, Markus Hunziker

Abstract
. An almost self-conjugate (ASC) partition has a Young diagram in which each arm along the diagonal is exactly one box longer than its corresponding leg. Classically, the ASC partitions and their conjugates appear in two of Littlewood’s symmetric function identities. These identities can be viewed as Euler characteristics of BGG complexes of the trivial representation, for classical Hermitian symmetric pairs. In this paper, we consider partitions in which the arm–leg difference is an arbitrary constant m m . Regarding these partitions and their conjugates as highest weights, we prove an identity yielding an infinite family of dimension equalities between 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n \mathfrak{gl}_{n} - and 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n + m \mathfrak{gl}_{n+m} -modules. We then interpret this combinatorial result in the context of blocks in parabolic category 𝒪 \mathcal{O} : using Enright–Shelton reduction, we find six infinite families of congruent blocks whose corresponding posets of highest weights consist of the partitions in question. These posets, in turn, lead to generalizations of the Littlewood identities and their corresponding BGG complexes. Our results in this paper shed light on the surprising combinatorics underlying the work of Enright and Willenbring (2004).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 05E10; Secondary 22E47, 17B10

1.
Introduction

1.1.
​​​

An
almost self-conjugate (ASC) partition
is a weakly decreasing tuple of positive integers whose Young diagram has a special shape: for each box on the main diagonal (marked with a dot in the figure below), its
arm
(the part of its row strictly to its right) is longer than its
leg
(the part of its column strictly below) by exactly one box. For example,
(7,5,4,2,1,1)
is an ASC partition, which we see from its Young diagram below (where the length of each arm is written to its right, and the length of each leg is written beneath it):

\scriptstyle\bullet

\scriptstyle\rightarrow

\scriptstyle 6

\scriptstyle\rightarrow

\scriptstyle 3

\scriptstyle\rightarrow

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle\downarrow

\scriptstyle\downarrow

\scriptstyle 0

\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle\downarrow

\scriptstyle 5

We follow
[
8
]
in adopting the “ASC” terminology; these partitions also play a role in a recent paper
[
22
]
under the name of “shift-symmetric” partitions. Partitions with the conjugate shape (i.e., where each leg is one box longer than its corresponding arm) are also known in the literature as “threshold partitions,” since they are precisely the partitions that can be realized as the degree sequence of a threshold graph; see
[
16
]
*Lemma 10. Our own interest in ASC partitions arose from their appearance in symmetric-function identities due to Dudley Littlewood, and in related BGG complexes. In a sense, these complexes are the “natural habitat” for ASC partitions and their conjugates. In this paper, we classify the BGG complexes acting as the natural habitat for a generalization of the ASC partitions and their conjugates, namely, partitions for which the arm–leg difference is an arbitrary constant
m
.

We begin by recalling three classical identities which will be a recurring theme of this paper, each identity involving the Schur polynomials
s_{\pi}
. First we have the dual Cauchy identity
[
28
]
*Thm. 7.14.3:

(I)

\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j})=\sum_{\mathclap{\pi\in\Par(p\times q)}}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})s_{\pi^{\prime}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{q}),

where
\Par(p\times q)
is the set of partitions whose Young diagram fits inside a
p\times q
rectangle. Next we have two of the Littlewood identities
[
23
]
*Section 11.9:

(II)

\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),

(III)

\displaystyle\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi^{\prime}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1}),

where each sum ranges over the ASC partitions with at most
n
parts. With these three identities in hand, we outline the results and methods of this paper below.

1.2.
Dimension identities

We begin by proving two new identities (Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
) relating the dimensions of certain modules for
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{n+m}
, when the highest weights are partitions whose arm–leg difference is
m
. In the special case
m=1
, the highest weights are precisely the ASC partitions appearing in the Littlewood identities (
II
) and (
III
). (See Figure
1
, which illustrates an example of the dimension identity in Theorem
2.2
.) These new dimension identities are interesting in their own right from a combinatorial viewpoint, but they play a larger role later in the paper, in the proof of our main result (Theorem
5.5
). This work arose from trying to understand when the ratio appearing in certain dimension identities in
[
15
]
is equal to 1.

1.3.
Generalized BGG resolutions

The identities (
I
)–(
III
) can be viewed as Euler characteristics of the Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand (BGG) complex for the trivial representation of each classical group. The following example is an informal preview.

(\mathsf{D}_{4},\mathsf{A}_{3})

\dim=1

Figure 1.
Two isomorphic posets from Example
1.1
. Note that the Young diagrams on the right-hand side are precisely the ASC partitions with at most
3
parts; meanwhile, each Young diagram on the left is the conjugate of its corresponding Young diagram on the right. On the left-hand side, each Young diagram represents the (dual of the)
\mathfrak{gl}_{4}
-module with corresponding highest weight; on the right-hand side, each Young diagram represents a
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
-module. Each diagram is labeled with the dimension of the corresponding
\mathfrak{gl}_{4}
- or
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
-module. By viewing the Young diagrams as parabolic Verma modules and the arrows as the canonical maps between them, one can interpret the left (resp., right) poset as the BGG complex of the trivial representation of
\mathfrak{so}_{4}
(resp.,
\mathfrak{sp}_{6}
).

Example 1.1
.

Throughout this example, we refer to Figure
1
, which shows two posets. The poset elements are highest weights for
\mathfrak{gl}_{4}
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
, respectively. On the left side we consider the Hermitian symmetric pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})=(\mathsf{D}_{4},\mathsf{A}_{3})=(\mathfrak{so}_{4},\mathfrak{gl}_{4})
; see Section
3.2
for the general theory of Hermitian symmetric pairs
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
and parabolic subalgebras
\mathfrak{q}
of Hermitian type. The poset shown on the left-hand side represents the BGG–Lepowsky complex of the trivial representation of
\mathfrak{so}_{4}
; that is to say, the complex is a free in terms of parabolic Verma modules
N_{\pi^{*}}\coloneqq U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{q})}F_{\pi^{*}}
, where
F_{\pi^{*}}
is the (dual of the) simple
\mathfrak{gl}_{4}
-module whose highest weight is the partition
\pi
. In the figure, we depict each parabolic Verma module
N_{\pi^{*}}
as the Young diagram of the partition
\pi
(decorated with the symbol
*
). The empty diagram
\bullet
on top therefore represents the first term
N_{0}
in the resolution, while the diagram just below it represents the second term
N_{(1,1)^{*}}
, and so forth. Several Young diagrams at the same level in the resolution should be understood as the direct sum of the corresponding parabolic Verma modules. Each arrow is the canonical map between parabolic Verma modules. We also label
the Young diagram of each
\pi
with the dimension of the corresponding
\mathfrak{gl}_{4}
-module
F_{\pi^{*}}
. Similarly, on the right-hand side of Figure
1
, we consider the Hermitian symmetric pair
(\mathsf{C}_{3},\mathsf{A}_{2})=(\mathfrak{sp}_{6},\mathfrak{gl}_{3})
. Just as on the left, the poset represents the BGG complex of the trivial representation of
\mathfrak{sp}_{6}
, where this time the Young diagram of
\pi
stands for the simple
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
-module
F_{\pi^{*}}
.

We first observe that the poset for
(\mathsf{D}_{4},\mathsf{A}_{3})
is clearly isomorphic to that for
(\mathsf{C}_{3},\mathsf{A}_{2})
. Second, we see that each partition appearing in the left-hand poset is the conjugate of its corresponding partition in the right-hand poset (i.e., the Young diagrams are transposes of each other). More specifically, the partitions
\pi
appearing on the right-hand side are precisely the ASC partitions with at most
3
parts, which are the partitions
\pi
occurring in the Littlewood identity (
II
). Likewise, their conjugate partitions
\pi^{\prime}
appearing on the left side are the partitions appearing in (
III
). Even without more details, it is not hard to believe that the alternating sum in (
II
) and (
III
) turns out to be the Euler characteristic of the BGG complex. (See
[
25
,
26
]
for a different approach to this fact.) We make another conspicuous observation: not only do the corresponding
\mathfrak{gl}_{4}
- and
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
-modules have highest weights that are conjugate to each other, but they also have the same dimension. This equality of dimensions is the aforementioned special case of Theorem
2.2
.

The informal observations above — namely, the isomorphism of posets which preserves BGG complexes and the dimension of the
\mathfrak{k}
-modules — describe a phenomenon that we will make rigorous in Section
5
, by means of the notion of
congruence of blocks
. Using this language, the upshot of the present example is that the principal blocks for
(\mathsf{D}_{n+1},\mathsf{A}_{n})
and
(\mathsf{C}_{n},\mathsf{A}_{n-1})
are congruent.

1.4.
Diagrams of Hermitian type

For us, the striking fact in Example
1.1
is that in this instance of congruence, the poset isomorphism is given by conjugate partitions. Our goal in this project was to find other congruences of blocks (in the context of Hermitian symmetric pairs) where the poset isomorphism is given by conjugate partitions. We thus build upon the work of Armour
[
1
]
, who observed the appearance of conjugate partitions in the context of congruence of singular and regular blocks. To illustrate the main idea behind Section
4
, we have shaded the Young diagrams in Figure
1
to show how they can be constructed via a “stacking” operation. On either side of Figure
1
, if we consider only the shaded boxes of the diagrams, then moving down along a line in the Hasse diagram adds one shaded box, such that the shaded boxes form a shifted Young diagram. (There is a natural way to identify these shifted Young diagrams with the lower-order ideals of the poset of positive noncompact roots of
\mathfrak{g}
, which in turn correspond to certain Weyl group elements.)
Note that the shaded part of each diagram is the same in both posets. On the right-hand side, to make the Young diagram of
\pi
itself, we “stack” the shaded diagram against the right edge of its reflection about the diagonal (the white boxes). This stacking construction by nature produces an ASC partition
\pi
.
Likewise, on the left-hand side of Figure
1
, each shaded diagram is stacked against the upper edge of its reflection, thereby producing the conjugate of an ASC partition.

1.5.
Congruence of blocks and conjugate partitions

Our main result (Theorem
5.5
, summarized in Table
2
) is a description of six infinite families of congruent blocks given by conjugate partitions, just as in Example
1.1
. The congruence in Example
1.1
is a special subfamily — in some sense, a degenerate case — in which the two resolutions are for finite-dimensional modules; in general, the complex on the left is the resolution of an infinite-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}
-module (see
[
15
]
). In verifying the six families of congruences in Table
2
, our primary tool is the process of
Enright–Shelton reduction
. This reduction, which has a strong combinatorial flavor, produces a poset isomorphism by deleting certain coordinates in a weight of
\mathfrak{g}
; the result of the reduction is therefore a weight of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
with generally smaller rank than
\mathfrak{g}
. (Even in Example
1.1
, we can see a sort of proto-Enright reduction from
\mathsf{D}_{4}
to
\mathsf{C}_{3}
.)

1.6.
Hilbert series and generalized Littlewood identities

As an application of our main result, we are able to write down the Hilbert series for the infinite-dimensional modules in each of our six families (see Table
3
). For certain of the families — namely, those in which the infinite-dimensional module is a Wallach representation of
\mathfrak{g}
— we thereby recover the well-known Hilbert series of determinantal varieties. By computing the Euler characteristic of BGG resolutions of the finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}
-modules, we derive six new families of identities (see Table
4
) generalizing the classical identities (
I
)–(
III
).

1.7.
Open problems

Our dimension identities in Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
raise further questions that we leave as open problems in Section
7
. In particular, one would like to find bijective proofs in terms of semistandard Young tableaux. It is also natural to look for other equalities among the dimensions of
\mathfrak{gl}
-modules, and to try to classify them. This problem is similar in flavor to that of classifying the equalities among the binomial coefficients, in the work of Lind
[
21
]
, Singmaster
[
27
]
, and de Weger
[
7
]
. Our main result in this paper also suggests the problem of classifying all instances of congruent blocks in the context of Hermitian symmetric pairs; see Figure
5
for one “sporadic” example lying outside the six families mentioned above.

2.
Dimension identities for
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
- and
\mathfrak{gl}_{n+m}
-modules

2.1.
Partitions

We use Greek letters to denote weakly decreasing tuples. In particular, a
partition
is a finite, weakly decreasing tuple of elements in
\mathbb{N}\coloneqq\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
. We regard two partitions as equal if they differ only by trailing zeros. Given a partition
\pi=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n})
, we write
|\pi|\coloneqq\sum_{i}\pi_{i}
.
We also make frequent use of the “dual” notation
\pi^{*}\coloneqq(-\pi_{n},\ldots,-\pi_{1})
.

To a partition
\pi
we associate a
Young diagram
, which consists of left-justified rows of boxes, such that the
i
th row from the top contains
\pi_{i}
boxes. Note that
|\pi|
is the number of boxes in the Young diagram of
\pi
. We define the
rank
, denoted by
\rk\pi
, to be the length of the main diagonal of the Young diagram of
\pi
. We write
\Par(p\times q)
for the set of partitions whose Young diagram fits inside a
p\times q
rectangle. The
conjugate
partition of
\pi
is the partition whose Young diagram is that of
\pi
reflected about the main diagonal; we write
\pi^{\prime}
to denote the conjugate of
\pi
(but see our disclaimer at the beginning of Section
5
). In diagrams, we use the symbol
\bullet
to denote the zero partition
0\coloneqq(0,\ldots,0)
, corresponding to the empty Young diagram.

A partition
\pi
of rank
r
can be uniquely described by its arm lengths
\alpha_{1}>\cdots>\alpha_{r}
and leg lengths
\beta_{1}>\cdots>\beta_{r}
, as follows. Define
\alpha_{i}
to be the number of boxes in the
i
th row strictly to the right of the main diagonal in the Young diagram of
\pi
; likewise, define
\beta_{i}
to be the number of boxes in the
i
th column strictly below the main diagonal. In this way, we will often denote a partition by its
Frobenius symbol
, writing

\pi=(\alpha|\beta)=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\mid\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r}).

If
\pi=0=(\;\mid\;)
, then
\alpha
and
\beta
are empty. Clearly if
\pi=(\alpha|\beta)
, then
\pi^{\prime}=(\beta|\alpha)
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we adopt the shorthand

\alpha+m\coloneqq(\alpha_{1}+m,\ldots,\alpha_{r}+m).

For example, if
\pi=(\alpha|\beta)
, then
(\alpha+m\mid\beta)
denotes the partition obtained by adding
m
to all the arms of
\pi
. We observe that an ASC partition, introduced informally in Section
1.1
, is a partition of the form
(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)
.

2.2.
Dimension identities

Throughout the paper, we let
F^{(n)}_{\mu}
denote the finite-dimensional simple
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-module with highest weight
\mu
, where
\mu
is a weakly decreasing
n
-tuple of integers. In the proof below, we will write
(i,j)\in\pi
to denote the box in the
i
th row (from the top) and
j
th column (from the left) of the Young diagram of a partition
\pi
. From each box
(i,j)\in\pi
there emanates a
hook
, which consists of all the boxes weakly to the right in row
i
or weakly below in column
j
. Thus the hook length
h(i,j)
is the number of boxes in the hook emanating from
(i,j)
. The content of a box is defined as
c(i,j)\coloneqq j-i
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(\alpha|\beta)\in\Par(p\times q)
. Then for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(2.1)

\dim F^{(p)}_{(\alpha+m\mid\beta)}\dim F^{(q)}_{(\beta+m|\alpha)}=\dim F^{(p+m)}_{(\alpha|\beta+m)}\dim F^{(q+m)}_{(\beta|\alpha+m)}.

Proof.

Suppose
\pi=(\alpha|\beta)
. By the hook–content formula
[
28
]
*Thm. 7.21.2, we have

(2.2)

\dim F^{(n)}_{\pi}=\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}\frac{n+c(i,j)}{h(i,j)}.

We first rewrite the product of the numerators in (
2.2
) in terms of hooks: letting
h_{k}
denote the hook emanating from box
(k,k)
, we have

\prod_{(i,j)\in h_{k}}(n+c(i,j))=\prod_{\ell=-\beta_{k}}^{\alpha_{k}}(n+\ell)=\frac{(n+\alpha_{k})!}{(n-\beta_{k}-1)!}

and so, putting
r=\rk\pi
, we have

(2.3)

\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}(n+c(i,j))=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{(n+\alpha_{k})!}{(n-\beta_{k}-1)!}.

Using (
2.2
) and (
2.3
), we can rewrite the left-hand side of (
2.1
) as

(2.4)

\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{(p+\alpha_{k}+m)!}{(p-\beta_{k}-1)!}}{\displaystyle\prod_{\mathclap{(i,j)\in(\alpha+m|\beta)}}h(i,j)}\>\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{(q+\beta_{k}+m)!}{(q-\alpha_{k}-1)!}}{\displaystyle\prod_{\mathclap{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in(\beta+m|\alpha)}}h(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})},

and the right-hand side of (
2.1
) as

(2.5)

\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{(p+m+\alpha_{k})!}{(p+m-\beta_{k}-m-1)!}}{\displaystyle\prod_{\mathclap{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in(\alpha|\beta+m)}}h(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\>\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{(q+m+\beta_{k})!}{(q+m-\alpha_{k}-m-1)!}}{\displaystyle\prod_{\mathclap{(i,j)\in(\beta|\alpha+m)}}h(i,j)}.

Clearly the numerator in (
2.4
) equals that in (
2.5
).
Moreover, since
(\alpha\mid\beta+m)=(\beta+m\mid\alpha)^{\prime}
, and since the multiset of hook lengths is preserved under conjugation of partitions, the denominators in (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) are also equal. Hence the left- and right-hand sides of (
2.1
) are equal, and the theorem follows.
∎

Theorem 2.2
.

We have
\dim F^{(n)}_{\pi}=\dim F^{(n+m)}_{\pi^{\prime}}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
if and only if
\pi
has the form
(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
.

Proof.

Suppose
\pi=(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
. In Theorem
2.1
, by setting
\alpha=\beta
and
p=q=n
, and then taking the square root of both sides of (
2.1
), we have
\dim F^{(n)}_{\pi}=\dim F^{(n+m)}_{\pi^{\prime}}
. Conversely, fix
m
and suppose that
\dim F^{(n)}_{\pi}=\dim F^{(n+m)}_{\pi^{\prime}}
for all
n
. Since conjugate partitions have the same multiset of hook lengths, and since
c(j,i)=-c(i,j)
, the hook–content formula (
2.2
) yields

\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}n+c(i,j)=\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}n+m-c(i,j).

Treating each side as a polynomial in
n
, by unique factorization we must have the equality of multisets

(2.6)

C_{1}\coloneqq\{c(i,j)\mid(i,j)\in\pi\}=\{m-c(i,j)\mid(i,j)\in\pi\}.

If
\pi=(\alpha|\beta)
, then
\alpha_{1}=\max(C_{1})
and
-\beta_{1}=\min(C_{1})
. But by (
2.6
), we must also have
\min(C_{1})=m-\alpha_{1}=-\beta_{1}
, and thus
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}+m
. Since the outermost hook
h_{1}
of
\pi
contains exactly one box with content
c
for each
-\beta_{1}\leq c\leq\alpha_{1}
, we delete these contents from
C_{1}
to obtain the new multiset

C_{2}\coloneqq C_{1}\setminus\{-\beta_{1},-\beta_{1}+1,\ldots,\alpha_{1}\}.

Just as before,
\alpha_{2}=\max(C_{2})
and
-\beta_{2}=\min(C_{2})=m-\alpha_{2}
, and so
\alpha_{2}=\beta_{2}+m
. Continuing in this way to define each new multiset
C_{i+1}\coloneqq C_{i}\setminus\{-\beta_{i},-\beta_{i}+1,\ldots,\alpha_{i}\}
, we obtain
\alpha_{i}=\beta_{i}+m
for each
i=1,\ldots,\rk\pi
.
Therefore
\pi
takes the form
(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

We also have the following
q
-analogue of Theorem
2.2
:

s_{\pi}(q^{n-1},q^{n-3},\ldots,q^{3-n},q^{1-n})=s_{\pi^{\prime}}(q^{n+m-1},q^{n+m-3},\ldots,q^{3-n-m},q^{1-n-m})

when
\pi=(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
. In terms of representation theory, this means that the modules in Theorem
2.2
not only have the same dimension, but are equivalent as
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules upon the restriction of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{n+m}
to their principal
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
subalgebra.

3.
Generalized BGG resolutions

3.1.
​​​

With Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
in mind, we recall the three classical identities (
I
), (
II
), and (
III
) from the introduction. We observe the similarity between (
I
) and Theorem
2.1
in the case
m=0
, where the two highest weights on the left-hand side of (
2.1
) are conjugates of each other, with the first being an element of
\Par(p\times q)
. Likewise the sums in the Littlewood identities (
II
) and (
III
) range over the same ASC partitions that appear as highest weights for the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-modules in Theorem
2.2
, in the case
m=1
. In the remainder of this section, we will explain how each of the identities (
I
), (
II
), and (
III
) can be viewed as the Euler characteristic of the BGG complex of the trivial representation; see also
[
25
,
26
]
for a different approach.

3.2.
Lie algebras of Hermitian type

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
be a real simple noncompact reductive Lie algebra, with Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{R}}
. We write the complexified Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
. From the general theory, there exists a distinguished element
h_{0}\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{k})
such that
\operatorname{ad}h_{0}
acts on
\mathfrak{g}
with eigenvalues
0
and
\pm 1
. We thus have a triangular decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}
, where
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid[h_{0},x]=\pm x\}
. The subalgebra
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is a maximal parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, with Levi subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
and abelian nilradical
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
. Parabolic subalgebras of complex simple Lie algebras that arise in this way are called parabolic subalgebras of
Hermitian type
, and
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
is called a
Hermitian symmetric pair
.

In this paper, we focus our attention on the three families of Hermitian symmetric pairs, which we call Types I, II, and III, that occur in the setting of Howe duality. In the list below, as we will do throughout the paper, we interchangeably name each pair according to the real Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbb{R}})
, the complexified Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
, and the Cartan classification:

\begin{array}[]{lccccc}\text{Type I:}&(\mathfrak{su}(p,q),\>\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{u}(p)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(q))&=&(\mathfrak{sl}_{p+q},\>\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{gl}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{q}))&=&(\mathsf{A}_{p+q-1},\>\mathsf{A}_{p-1}\times\mathsf{A}_{q-1}).\\
\text{Type II:}&(\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\>\mathfrak{u}(n))&=&(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n},\>\mathfrak{gl}_{n})&=&(\mathsf{C}_{n},\>\mathsf{A}_{n-1}).\\
\text{Type III:}&(\mathfrak{so}^{*}(2n),\>\mathfrak{u}(n))&=&(\mathfrak{so}_{2n},\>\mathfrak{gl}_{n})&=&(\mathsf{D}_{n},\>\mathsf{A}_{n-1}).\end{array}

For each type above, we give explicit realizations of
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{k}
, and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
in the first three columns of Table
1
. In the
\mathfrak{g}
column, for Type I,
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is a block
(p+q)\times(p+q)
complex matrix, while for Types II and III it is a block
2n\times 2n
complex matrix. In the
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
column, we write
\M_{p,q}
for the space of complex
p\times q
matrices, while
\SM_{n}
(resp.,
\AM_{n}
) denotes the symmetric (resp., alternating) complex
n\times n
matrices. In the next column, we write down the character of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]
as a
\mathfrak{k}
-module, setting
x_{i}\coloneqq e^{-\varepsilon_{i}}
and
y_{j}\coloneqq e^{\varepsilon_{j}}
. (For Type I, the sum ranges over
1\leq i\leq p
and
1\leq j\leq q
, whereas in Types II and III we have
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq i<j\leq n
, respectively.) We also include the well-known expansions of these characters in terms of Schur polynomials: in Type I, this yields the Cauchy identity, while in Types II and III the identities were recorded by Littlewood
[
23
]
*Section 11.9 on the same page as his identities (
II
) and (
III
). In the last column of Table
1
, by the phrase “even rows (resp., columns),” we mean that all row (resp., column) lengths in the Young diagram are even.

Type

\mathfrak{g}

\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{smallmatrix}\right]\>\middle|\>\ldots\right\}

\mathfrak{k}

\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right\}

\mathfrak{p}^{+}

\left\{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&B\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right\}

\ch\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]=\ch S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})

\nu\in\ldots

[2pt]
I

\mathfrak{sl}_{p+q}

\tr A+\tr D=0

\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{gl}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{q})

\M_{p,q}

\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}=\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu}(\mathbf{y})

\Par(\min\{p,q\},\infty)

II

\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}

A=-D^{t},B=B^{t},C=C^{t}

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

\SM_{n}

\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}=\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})

\Par(n,\infty),

even rows

III

\mathfrak{so}_{2n}

A=-D^{t},B=-B^{t},C=-C^{t}

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

\AM_{n}

\displaystyle\prod_{i<j}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}=\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})

\Par(n,\infty),

even columns

Table 1.
Summary of data for Hermitian symmetric pairs of Types I, II, and III. For Type I, we write
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{q})
; for the other types,
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

3.3.
Roots and weights

Suppose
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
is a Hermitian symmetric pair, and let
\mathfrak{h}
be a Cartan subalgebra of both
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{k}
. Let
\Phi
be the root system of the pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, and
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
the root space corresponding to
\alpha\in\Phi
. Then put
\Phi(\mathfrak{k})=\{\alpha\in\Phi\mid\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}\}
and
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})=\{\alpha\in\Phi\mid\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}^{+}\}
.
Choose a set
\Phi^{+}
of positive roots so that
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\subseteq\Phi^{+}
, and let
\Phi^{-}=-\Phi^{+}
denote the negative roots. We write
\Phi^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
for
\Phi^{+}\cap\Phi(\mathfrak{k})
. Let
\Pi=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\}\subset\Phi^{+}
denote the set of simple roots. We write
\langle\;,\;\rangle
to denote the nondegenerate bilinear form on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
induced from the Killing form of
\mathfrak{g}
. For
\alpha\in\Phi
, we write
\alpha^{\vee}\coloneqq 2\alpha/\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
. We define the fundamental roots
\omega_{i}
such that they form a basis of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
dual to the
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
, i.e.,
\langle\omega_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
. As usual, we let
\rho\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\alpha
. Explicitly, we have the following, where
\rho
is expressed in the standard
\varepsilon
-coordinates:

Type I:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq p\>\text{ or }\>p+1\leq i<j\leq p+q\},

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq p\>\text{
and }\>p+1\leq j\leq p+q\},

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=\{\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}\mid 1\leq i\leq p+q-1\},

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=(p+q-1,p+q-2,\ldots,2,1,0).

Type II:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\},

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq j\leq n\}.

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=\{\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}\mid 1\leq i\leq n-1\}\cup\{\alpha_{n}=2\varepsilon_{n}\},

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=(n,n-1,\ldots,3,2,1).

Type III:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\},

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\},

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=\{\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}\mid 1\leq i\leq n-1\}\cup\{\alpha_{n}=\varepsilon_{n-1}+\varepsilon_{n}\},

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i},&1\leq i\leq n-2,\\
\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{n-1}-\varepsilon_{n}),&i=n-1,\\
\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{n}),&i=n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,2,1,0).

Note that
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
inherits the usual poset structure from
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, where
\mu\leq\lambda
if and only if
\lambda-\mu
can be written as an
\mathbb{N}
-linear combination of positive roots. There is a unique element of
\Pi\cap\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
, the “noncompact simple root,” which is the smallest element of the poset
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be the Weyl group of the pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, and
\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{k})\subseteq\mathcal{W}
the Weyl group of the pair
(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{h})
. For each
w\in\mathcal{W}
, let
\Phi_{w}\coloneqq\Phi^{+}\cap w\Phi^{-}
. Then we have the usual length function
\ell
on
\mathcal{W}
, whereby

(3.1)

\ell(w)=|\Phi_{w}|.

Following Kostant
[
18
]
*(5.13.1), we define

\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}\coloneqq\{w\in\mathcal{W}\mid\Phi_{w}\subseteq\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\},

the subset of minimal-length right coset representatives of
\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{k})
in
\mathcal{W}
. To refine
\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
by length, we further define

\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}_{i}\coloneqq\{w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}\mid\ell(w)=i\}.

The “dot” action by the Weyl group is defined as follows:

w\cdot\lambda\coloneqq w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho

for
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. We define the reflection
s_{\alpha}:\mathfrak{h}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}^{*}
by
s_{\alpha}(\lambda)\coloneqq\lambda-\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\alpha
. Let

\displaystyle\Lambda^{+}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}\mid\langle\lambda+\rho,\>\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\},

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}\mid\langle\lambda+\rho,\>\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})\}

denote the sets of dominant integral weights with respect to
\Phi^{+}
and
\Phi^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
, respectively. In Type I, we write a weight in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
as a
(p+q)
-tuple in which a semicolon separates the first
p
coordinates from the last
q
coordinates. Upon restriction to
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{gl}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{q})
, a weight
(\mu;\nu)
is thus the ordered pair
(\mu,\nu)
. When
(\mu,\nu)\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
, we will also write
\mu\otimes\nu
, since it is the highest weight of the irreducible
\mathfrak{k}
-module
F^{(p)}_{\mu}\otimes F^{(q)}_{\nu}
. In Types II and III, elements of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
are written as
n
-tuples.

From the general theory of Hermitian symmetric pairs, we have an isomorphism of posets

(3.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}&\cong\{\text{lower-order ideals in }\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\},\\
w&\mapsto\Phi_{w},\end{split}

with
\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
a poset under the Bruhat order, and the set of lower-order ideals in
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
ordered by inclusion, with the usual ordering on the roots. Hence
\Phi_{w}
can be pictured as a subdiagram of the Hasse diagram of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
, which can be drawn on a square lattice.

3.4.
BGG–Lepowsky resolutions

For
\mu\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
, let
F_{\mu}
be the finite-dimensional simple
\mathfrak{k}
-module with highest weight
\mu
. Then
F_{\mu}
is also a module for
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}
, with
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
acting by zero. We define the
parabolic Verma module

(3.3)

N_{\lambda}\coloneqq U(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{q})}F_{\lambda}.

When
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
is a Hermitian symmetric pair,
\mathfrak{p}^{-}
is abelian and therefore we can identify
U(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
with
S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
. By the PBW theorem, we thus obtain

N_{\lambda}\cong S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\otimes F_{\lambda}

as a
\mathfrak{k}
-module. It will therefore be easy for us to write down the
\mathfrak{k}
-character of a parabolic Verma module, as follows. Since
\mathfrak{p}^{-}\cong(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{*}
, we have
S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{p}^{+}]
, and so we can find
\ch S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
listed in Table
1
. In Types II and III, where
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
, if
\mu^{*}
happens to be a partition, then
\ch F_{\mu}=s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})
. In Type I, if
\mu^{*}
and
\nu
both happen to be partitions, then
\ch F_{\mu\otimes\nu}=s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu}(\mathbf{y})
. (Recall that the symbol
\mu^{*}
denotes the tuple obtained by negating and reversing the coordinates of
\mu
.)

Let
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}
, and let
L_{\lambda}
be the finite-dimensional simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module with highest weight
\lambda
. Generalizing the Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand (BGG) resolution in terms of ordinary Verma modules
[
2
]
, Lepowsky
[
20
]
showed that there exists a resolution for
L_{\lambda}
in terms of parabolic Verma modules:

0\longrightarrow N_{s}\longrightarrow N_{s-1}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{0}\longrightarrow L_{\lambda}\longrightarrow 0,

where

(3.4)

N_{i}=\bigoplus_{\mathclap{w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}_{i}}}N_{w\cdot\lambda}

and
s=|\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})|
. We now state the result (to be proved in Section
4
) that connects these BGG resolutions (where
\lambda=0
) with the three classical identities (
I
)–(
III
) above:

Proposition 3.1
.

In each of Types I–III, the trivial
\mathfrak{g}
-module
\mathbbm{1}
has the generalized BGG resolution

0\longrightarrow N_{s}\longrightarrow N_{s-1}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{1}\longrightarrow 0,

with the terms given as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\text{{Type I}}&(\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{A}_{p+q-1}):&\displaystyle N_{i}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\Par(p\times q),\\
|\pi|=i\end{subarray}}N_{\pi^{*}\otimes\pi^{\prime}}&\text{{and }}s=pq.\\
\text{{Type II}}&(\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{C}_{n}):&\displaystyle N_{i}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(\alpha+1|\alpha),\\
\alpha_{1}<n,\\
|\alpha+1|=i\end{subarray}}N_{\pi^{*}}&\text{{and }}s=\binom{n+1}{2}.\\
\text{{Type III}}&(\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{n+1}):&\displaystyle N_{i}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(\alpha+1|\alpha),\\
\alpha_{1}<n,\\
|\alpha+1|=i\end{subarray}}N_{\pi^{\prime*}}&\text{{and }}s=\binom{n+1}{2}.\par\end{array}

(In Type III, we have stated the proposition for
\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
rather than
\mathsf{D}_{n}
, in order to line up our results with the Littlewood identity (
III
) in
n+1
variables. We have added the extra variable so that the sums in both Littlewood identities would range over the same set of partitions.) Observe that
|(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)|=2|\alpha+1|
; then by alternating the characters of the terms in the BGG resolutions in Proposition
3.1
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{{Type I:}}&\displaystyle 1=\ch\mathbbm{1}=\ch S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\cdot\sum_{\mathclap{\pi\in\Par(p\times q)}}(-1)^{|\pi|}\ch\left(F^{(p)}_{\pi^{*}}\otimes F^{(q)}_{\pi^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{|\pi|}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})s_{\pi^{\prime}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{q})}{\prod_{i,j}(1-x_{i}y_{j})}.\\
\text{{Type II:}}&\displaystyle 1=\ch\mathbbm{1}=\ch S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\cdot\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(\alpha+1|\alpha),\\
\alpha_{1}<n\end{subarray}}}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}\ch F^{(n)}_{\pi^{*}}=\frac{\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}{\prod_{i\leq j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})}.\\
\text{{Type III:}}&\displaystyle 1=\ch\mathbbm{1}=\ch S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\cdot\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(\alpha+1|\alpha),\\
\alpha_{1}<n\end{subarray}}}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}\ch F^{(n+1)}_{\pi^{\prime*}}=\frac{\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi^{\prime}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})}{\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})}.\end{array}

For Type I, rearranging the equation above yields the dual Cauchy identity (
I
) under the substitution
x_{i}\mapsto-x_{i}
. For Types II and III, rearrangement yields the Littlewood identities (
II
) and (
III
).

Remark 3.2
.

In Types II and III, by looking at the
graded
characters of parabolic Verma modules in the parabolic BGG resolution of the trivial representation, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\operatorname{ASC}}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}t^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}{\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j}(1-tx_{i}x_{j})}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\operatorname{ASC}}(-1)^{|\pi|/2}t^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi^{\prime}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}{\displaystyle\prod_{i<j}(1-tx_{i}x_{j})}

\displaystyle=1.

Rearranging and replacing
t
with
-t
, we obtain

\displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j}(1+tx_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\operatorname{ASC}}t^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),

\displaystyle\prod_{i<j}(1+tx_{i}x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\operatorname{ASC}}t^{|\pi|/2}s_{\pi^{\prime}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}).

4.
Diagrams of Hermitian type

4.1.
​​​

In this section, we introduce certain diagrams that encode the highest weights
w\cdot\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
occurring in the BGG resolutions. We will then be able to write down all of these weights directly from the diagrams, eliminating the need for calculations in terms of the Weyl group. This will allow for a quick proof of Proposition
3.1
above. Moreover, in Types II and III, it turns out that these diagrams are ASC partitions and their conjugates.

4.2.
A recursive formula for
w\cdot\lambda

We follow the exposition in
[
9
]
*Section 3.7, to which we refer the reader for details; we emphasize that the following facts are valid only within the Hermitian symmetric setting. Suppose
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
is a Hermitian symmetric pair. Then there exists a unique map
f:\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\longrightarrow\Pi
, such that

(4.1)

\Phi_{w}=\Phi_{v}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}\quad(w,v\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad w=vs_{f(\beta)}=s_{{}_{\beta}}v.

In this case, we have the explicit formula

(4.2)

f(\beta)=v^{-1}\beta.

It follows inductively that if we write
\Phi_{w}=\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\}
such that for every
i=1,\ldots,\ell
the set
\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{i}\}
is a lower-order ideal of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
, then

(4.3)

w=s_{f(\beta_{1})}\cdots s_{f(\beta_{\ell})}=s_{\beta_{\ell}}\cdots s_{\beta_{1}}.

In this paper, the motivation behind the map
f
is the following observation (generalizing a result of Kostant in the case
\lambda=0
):

Lemma 4.1
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
and
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
, we have

(4.4)

w\cdot\lambda=\lambda-\sum_{\mathclap{\beta\in\Phi_{w}}}\big\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\beta)^{\vee}\big\rangle\beta.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
\ell(w)
. In the base case
w={\rm id}
, we have
\Phi_{w}=\varnothing
and so the sum in (
4.4
) is empty, as desired.
Now let
v\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
and assume that (
4.4
) holds for all elements in
\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
with length at most
\ell(v)
. Let
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\Phi_{w}=\Phi_{v}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}
; then
\ell(w)=\ell(v)+1
. From (
4.1
) we have
w=vs_{f(\beta)}
, and so

\displaystyle w\cdot\lambda

\displaystyle=w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho

\displaystyle=vs_{f(\beta)}(\lambda+\rho)-\rho

\displaystyle=v\Big(\lambda+\rho-\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\beta)^{\vee}\rangle f(\beta)\Big)-\rho

\displaystyle=v(\lambda+\rho)-\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\beta)^{\vee}\rangle v\big(f(\beta)\big)-\rho

\displaystyle=v\cdot\lambda-\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\beta)^{\vee}\rangle\beta,

where in the last equality we have used (
4.2
) to obtain
v(f(\beta))=vv^{-1}\beta=\beta
. By the induction hypothesis, we have
v\cdot\lambda=\lambda-\sum_{\gamma\in\Phi_{v}}\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\gamma)^{\vee}\rangle\gamma
. Since
\Phi_{w}=\Phi_{v}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}
, we see that
w
satisfies (
4.4
).
∎

4.3.
Diagrams of Hermitian type

The proof of Lemma
4.1
suggests a recursive method for computing
w\cdot\lambda
, supposing that we know the reduced expression
w=s_{\beta_{\ell}}\cdots s_{\beta_{1}}
on the right-hand side of (
4.3
). We can do even better, however: in this section, we interpret Lemma
4.1
diagrammatically, which will enable us to write down
w\cdot\lambda
directly in terms of
\Phi_{w}
.

Given
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}
, we will adopt two useful methods for abbreviating the inner product
\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\beta)^{\vee}\rangle
; the first method is in terms of
\beta
, while the second method is in terms of the simple root
f(\beta)
. For the first method, we set the following shorthand for the roots in
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
:

\beta_{ij}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{p+j},&\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of Type I }(1\leq i\leq p,1\leq j\leq q),\\
\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j},&\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of Type II }(n\geq i\geq j\geq 1),\\
\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j},&\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of Type III }(n\geq i>j\geq 1).\end{cases}

Then using two indices, we write

(4.5)

d_{ij}\coloneqq\big\langle\lambda+\rho,\>f(\beta_{ij})^{\vee}\big\rangle,

which allows us to rewrite Lemma
4.1
as

(4.6)

w\cdot\lambda=\lambda-\sum_{\mathclap{\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}}}d_{ij}\beta_{ij}.

For the second method, we use just a single index, writing

(4.7)

d_{i}\coloneqq\big\langle\lambda+\rho,\>\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\big\rangle.

In order to pass between the two abbreviations, we have the following formulas (see
[
9
]
*appendix):

(4.8)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Type I:}&d_{ij}=d_{i+j-1}.\\
\text{Type II:}&d_{ij}=d_{n-i+j}.\\[8.61108pt]
\text{Type III:}&d_{ij}=\begin{cases}d_{n},&i-j=1\text{ with }n-i\text{ even},\\
d_{n-i+j}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}

For a fixed
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}
, we now associate to each
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
a diagram as follows (see Figures
2
and
3
):

•

Orient the Hasse diagram of
\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
so that the noncompact simple root is in the northwest corner, the highest root is in the southeast corner, and
\beta_{pq}
(Type I) or
\beta_{n1}
(Types II and III) is in the northeast corner.

•

Form a Young diagram
[\Phi_{w}]
by placing a box at each root in
\Phi_{w}
. In Type I, we obtain the (true) Young diagram of some partition in
\Par(p\times q)
. In Types II and III, we obtain a shifted Young diagram in which the row lengths are strictly decreasing.

•

In the diagram
[\Phi_{w}]
, fill the box corresponding to
\beta_{ij}
with the entry
d_{ij}
. Since the entries
d_{ij}
depend on
\lambda
, we denote this filled diagram by the symbol
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
, and call it a
diagram of Hermitian type
.

We will treat
[\Phi_{w}]
as both an unfilled diagram and as the partition given by its row lengths; in particular,
[\Phi_{w}]_{i}
is the number of boxes in the
i
th row. We observe from (
3.1
) that

(4.9)

\ell(w)=\big|[\Phi_{w}]\big|.

In Figure
2
, we display
[\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})]_{\lambda}
filled with the entries
d_{ij}
, while in Figure
3
we use (
4.8
) to display the equivalent filling with the
d_{i}
. (The
\varepsilon
’s along the margins are merely a visual aid.) It follows from (
4.8
) that the entries
d_{i}
are constant along each diagonal; hence in Figure
3
, the diagonal dots represent the same entry that is in the boxes directly northwest and southeast. (The only exception to this is the main diagonal in Type III, where the entries alternate between
d_{n}
and
d_{n-1}
.)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}*(gray!40)\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to20.39996pt{\vss\hbox to20.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-20.39996pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=20.39996pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 20.00003pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=20.39996pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-20.39996pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 20.00003pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to20.39996pt{\vss\hbox to20.00003pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-\varepsilon_{p+1}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-20.39996pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=20.39996pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 20.00003pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=20.39996pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-20.39996pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 20.00003pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox Figure 3.
The diagram
[\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})]_{\lambda}
of Hermitian type, filled with the
d_{i}
as in (
4.7
).

4.4.
Stacking diagrams

We now introduce a “stacking” construction that converts
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
into a new diagram
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
with twice the size. Ultimately we will treat the diagram
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
as mere shorthand for the stacked diagram
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
, which is the actual key to writing down the highest weights
w\cdot\lambda
. First we define an unfilled diagram
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
via its row lengths:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{Type I:}&\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{i}=[\Phi_{w}]^{\prime}_{-(q+1-i)},&\text{ for }1\leq i\leq q,\\
&\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{q+i}=[\Phi_{w}]_{i},&\text{ for }1\leq i\leq p.\\
\text{Type II:}&\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{i}=[\Phi_{w}]_{i}+[\Phi_{w}]^{\prime}_{i},&\text{ for }1\leq i\leq n.\\
\text{Type III:}&\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket^{\prime}_{i}=[\Phi_{w}]_{i}+[\Phi_{w}]^{\prime}_{i},&\text{ for }1\leq i\leq n-1.\end{array}

For the first
q
rows in Type I, the negative row lengths mean that the rows extend to the left instead of to the right.
We illustrate the construction below. In Type I, the shaded diagram
[\Phi_{w}]=(5,3,2)
is stacked corner-to-corner with its reflection about the 45-degree axis:

\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower In the case above, the row lengths from top to bottom are
(-1,-1,-2,-3,-3;5,3,2)
. Note that we have written the semicolon after the first
q
coordinates (i.e., the row lengths of the reflected diagram), but ultimately, in light of Theorem
4.5
, we will work with the dual tuple
(-2,-3,-5;3,3,2,1,1)
, where as usual the semicolon is written after the first
p
coordinates. In general for Type I, if
[\Phi_{w}]=\pi\in\Par(p\times q)
, then we have the following description of
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
as a
(q+p)
-tuple of its row lengths:

(4.10)

\text{Type I:}\quad\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=(\pi^{\prime*};\pi).

In Type II (resp., Type III), the shaded diagram
[\Phi_{w}]=(5,3,2)
is stacked horizontally (resp., vertically) with its conjugate:

height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\par\\
\text{Type II}&&\text{Type III}\end{array}

If we modify the Frobenius notation for Types II and III to write
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|0)
, where the “0” records the fact that all leg lengths in a shifted diagram are zero, then we can describe the stacking construction in terms of ASC partitions:

(4.11)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Type II:}&\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=(\alpha+1\mid\alpha).\\
\text{Type III:}&\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=(\alpha\mid\alpha+1).\end{array}

In the same way that we constructed the unfilled diagram
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
from
[\Phi_{w}]
, we can construct a filled diagram
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
from
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
. Specifically, the shape of
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
is given by
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
, while the filling is induced by the filling of
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
. In Type I, we fill the reflection of
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
with the negatives of the original entries, which we denote by a bar (e.g.,
\overline{3}=-3
). Given a filled diagram
D
, we write
\rows D
(resp.
\cols D
) to denote the tuple whose
i
th coordinate is the sum of the entries in the
i
th row (resp., column) of
D
, counting from top to bottom (resp., left to right). We then observe the following:

(4.12)

\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}\left(\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]^{*}_{\lambda};\>\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}\right),&\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of Type I},\\[4.30554pt]
\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}+\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda},&\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of Type II},\\[4.30554pt]
(\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda},\>0)+(0,\>\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}),&\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of Type III,}\end{cases}

where
(-,0)
and
(0,-)
denote the
n
-tuples obtained by appending and prepending a
0
, respectively.

Example 4.2

(Type I)
.

Let
p=3
and
q=4
. Let
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\Phi_{w}=\{\beta_{31},\beta_{32},\beta_{33},\beta_{34},\beta_{21},\beta_{22},\beta_{11}\}
. (Although we will not need to work with
w
directly, it is easy enough to see that
w=s_{11}s_{22}s_{21}s_{34}s_{33}s_{32}s_{31}
, where
s_{ij}\coloneqq s_{\beta_{ij}}
). By consulting Figure
2
, we see that
[\Phi_{w}]
is the partition
(4,2,1)
. Now fix
\lambda=(3,3,3;0,0,0,0)
. Then
\lambda+\rho=(9,8,7;3,2,1,0)
. Hence
d_{1}=d_{2}=d_{4}=d_{5}=d_{6}=1
, while
d_{3}=4
. Therefore, following the diagram in Figure
3
, we have

Example 4.3

(Type II)
.

Let
n=4
. Let
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\Phi_{w}=\{\beta_{44},\beta_{43},\beta_{42},\beta_{41},\beta_{33}\}
.
By (
4.3
) we have
w=s_{33}s_{41}s_{42}s_{43}s_{44}
. Then
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|0)
where
\alpha=(3,0)
. Now fix
\lambda=(9,5,3,3)
. Then
\lambda+\rho=(13,8,5,4)
. Hence
d_{1}=5
,
d_{2}=3
,
d_{3}=1
, and
d_{4}=4
. Therefore we have

[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}*(gray!30)\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox .

Example 4.4

(Type III)
.

Let
n=4
. Let
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\Phi_{w}=\{\beta_{43},\beta_{42},\beta_{41},\beta_{32},\beta_{31}\}
.
By (
4.3
) we have
w=s_{31}s_{32}s_{41}s_{42}s_{43}
. Then
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|0)
where
\alpha=(2,1)
. Now fix
\lambda=\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2},-\frac{3}{2}\right)
. Then
\lambda+\rho=(\frac{9}{2},\frac{7}{2},\frac{5}{2},-\frac{3}{2})
. Hence
d_{1}=d_{2}=d_{4}=1
, while
d_{3}=4
. Therefore we have

[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}*(gray!30)\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 4$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule 690.4pt}}$}\qquad\leadsto\qquad\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}

from which we see that
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}=(3,6,5,2)
. Note also that
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=(2,1\mid 3,2)=(\alpha\mid\alpha+1)
.

4.5.
BGG resolutions from the diagrams

We arrive at the main result of this section:

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
is of Type I, II, or III. Let
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
and
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
. Then we have

w\cdot\lambda=\lambda+{\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}^{*}}_{\textstyle.}

Proof.

The arguments below are clear from the diagrams in Figure
2
:

Type I:
We have

\Sigma\coloneqq\sum_{\mathclap{\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}}}d_{ij}\beta_{ij}=\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j):\\
\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}\end{subarray}}}d_{ij}(\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{p+j})=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\overbrace{\left(\sum_{j}d_{ij}\right)}^{\mathclap{\text{$i$th row sum from the bottom}}}\varepsilon_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i}d_{ij}\right)}_{\mathclap{\text{$j$th column sum}}}\varepsilon_{p+j}

where the row and column sums refer to
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
. Therefore the first
p
coordinates of
\Sigma
are those of
\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
in reverse order, while the final
q
coordinates of
\Sigma
are those of
-\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
. Hence by (
4.12
), the coordinates of
\Sigma
are the coordinates of
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
in reverse order. The result follows from (
4.6
).

Type II:
We have

\Sigma\coloneqq\sum_{\mathclap{\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}}}d_{ij}\beta_{ij}=\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j):\\
\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}\end{subarray}}}d_{ij}(\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overbrace{\left(\sum_{j}d_{ij}\right)}^{\mathclap{\text{$i$th row sum from the bottom}}}\varepsilon_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i}d_{ij}\right)}_{\mathclap{\text{$j$th column sum from the right}}}\varepsilon_{j}

where the row and column sums refer to
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
. Therefore the coordinates of
\Sigma
are those of
\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}+\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
in reverse order. By (
4.12
), these are also the coordinates of
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
in reverse order. The result follows from (
4.6
).

Type III:
We have

\Sigma\coloneqq\sum_{\mathclap{\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}}}d_{ij}\beta_{ij}=\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j):\\
\beta_{ij}\in\Phi_{w}\end{subarray}}}d_{ij}(\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j})=\sum_{i=2}^{n}\overbrace{\left(\sum_{j}d_{ij}\right)}^{\mathclap{\text{$(i-1)$th row sum from the bottom}}}\varepsilon_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i}d_{ij}\right)}_{\mathclap{\text{$j$th column sum from the right}}}\varepsilon_{j}

where the row and column sums refer to
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
. Therefore the coordinates of
\Sigma
are those of

(\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda},\>0)+(0,\>\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda})

in reverse order. By (
4.12
), these are also the coordinates of
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
in reverse order. The result follows from (
4.6
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We must show that the weights
w\cdot 0
are precisely those appearing in the resolutions in Proposition
3.1
. When
\lambda=0
, we have
d_{i}=\langle\rho,\>\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle=1
for all
i
. Hence in this case, each box in
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda}
is filled with a “1,” and therefore
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{0}=\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
.

In Type I, we have a bijection
\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow\Par(p\times q)
given by
w\longmapsto[\Phi_{w}]
. Setting
\pi=[\Phi_{w}]\in\Par(p\times q)
, we combine Theorem
4.5
with (
4.10
) to obtain

w\cdot 0=\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket^{*}=(\pi^{\prime*};\pi)^{*}=(\pi^{*};\pi^{\prime}).

In Type II, we have a bijection between
\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
and strictly decreasing partitions
\alpha
such that
n>\alpha_{1}>\cdots>\alpha_{r}
with
r\leq n
; the bijection is given by
w\longmapsto[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|0)
. Setting
\pi=\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
, we combine Theorem
4.5
with (
4.11
) to obtain

w\cdot 0=\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket^{*}=\pi^{*}=(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)^{*}.

The argument is the same in Type III (for
\mathsf{D}_{n+1})
if we set
\pi^{\prime}=\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
.

It remains to show that each individual term
N_{i}
in the resolutions of Proposition
3.1
contains the correct partitions
\pi
, but in all three types this is immediate from (
4.9
). The values of
s=|\Phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+})|
are easily calculated in each type.
∎

In order to interpret this as a map between parabolic Verma modules, we apply Theorem
4.5
, which directs us to read off the tuple of row sums for each stacked diagram, and then add its dual to
\lambda
:

\displaystyle\lambda+(-3,-5;4,4)^{*}

\displaystyle=(6,3;3,1)+(-4,-4;5,3)

\displaystyle=(2,-1;8,4),

\displaystyle\lambda+(0,-5;1,4)^{*}

\displaystyle=(6,3;3,1)+(-4,-1;5,0)

\displaystyle=(2,2;8,1).

Therefore the map in (
4.13
) is the map
N_{(2,-1;8,4)}\longrightarrow N_{(2,2;8,1)}
in the BGG resolution of
L_{\lambda}
.

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}

Applying Theorem
4.5
, we compute the row sums and add the dual to
\lambda
:

\displaystyle\lambda+(8,6,4)^{*}

\displaystyle=(2,0,0)-(4,6,8)

\displaystyle=(-2,-6,-8),

\displaystyle\lambda+(8,5,3)^{*}

\displaystyle=(2,0,0)-(3,5,8)

\displaystyle=(-1,-5,-8).

Therefore the map in (
4.14
) is the map
N_{(8,6,2)^{*}}\longrightarrow N_{(8,5,1)^{*}}
in the BGG resolution of
L_{\lambda}
.

Remark 4.8
.

Suppose we fix an origin on
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
, at the common corner (Type I) or the northwest corner (Types II and III). If we adopt the convention (as in Type I) that boxes in the right (resp., left) half-plane contain positive (resp., negative) entries, then taking the dual of
\rows\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
is equivalent to first rotating
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
by 180 degrees about the origin.

5.
Congruence of blocks and conjugate partitions

5.1.
​​​

This section is the heart of the paper. Our goal is to classify the occurrences of the phenomena we observed in Example
1.1
. In particular, we wish to find (pairs of) pairs
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
and
(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
of Types I–III, along with
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
and
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
, such that the highest weights
\{\mu=w\cdot\lambda\mid w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}\}
and
\{\mu^{\prime}=w^{\prime}\cdot\lambda^{\prime}\mid w^{\prime}\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}\}
form two isomorphic posets; moreover, this poset isomorphism should preserve BGG resolutions, and also preserve the dimension of each
\mathfrak{k}
-module
F_{\mu}
. Finally, this isomorphism should send Young diagrams of (duals of) poset elements to their conjugate diagrams. Our main result, namely Theorem
5.5
and its summary in Table
2
, consists of six infinite families that enjoy the properties observed in the pair from Example
1.1
.

A word of warning: in this section, we use the prime symbol to denote the image of a weight under a certain reduction operation. Hence,
a priori
the symbol
\mu^{\prime}
now has nothing to do with the conjugate partition of
\mu
. Nonetheless, it will turn out that in the special settings of Theorem
5.5
, certain poset elements
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
truly are conjugate partitions, as suggested by the notation.

One major advantage of our methods in this paper is that they eliminate the need for explicit calculations in the Weyl group; as a result, until now we have not even needed to write down the actions of the reflections
s_{ij}\coloneqq s_{\beta_{ij}}
. In this section, however, it will be useful to record the following. In Type I,
s_{ij}
transposes the
i
th and
(p+j)
th coordinates. In Types II and III,
s_{ij}
transposes and negates the
i
th and
j
th coordinates. In Type II, we also have
s_{ii}
, which negates the
i
th coordinate.

5.2.
Congruent blocks in parabolic category
\mathcal{O}

Recall the parabolic Verma module
N_{\lambda}
from (
3.3
). We define
L_{\lambda}
to be the unique simple quotient of
N_{\lambda}
. We now introduce a category in which these modules
N_{\lambda}
and
L_{\lambda}
are the basic objects. Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
be a Hermitian symmetric pair. We denote by
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
the full subcategory of
U(\mathfrak{g})
-mod whose objects belong to following set:

\left\{M\>\middle|\>\parbox{170.71652pt}{$M$ is a finitely generated $U(\mathfrak{g})$-module,\\
$M$ is a locally finite $U(\mathfrak{q})$-module,\\
$M$ is a semisimple $U(\mathfrak{k})$-module}\right\}.

The simple modules in
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
are parametrized by
\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
, via the correspondence
L_{\lambda}\longleftrightarrow\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
. For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
, let
\chi_{\lambda}
be the infinitesimal character of the ordinary Verma module
M_{\lambda}
, and therefore of its quotients
N_{\lambda}
and
L_{\lambda}
. We let
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})_{\lambda}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
whose objects are the modules whose composition factors have the infinitesimal character
\chi_{\lambda}
. Given
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
, the category
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})_{\lambda}
contains finitely many simple modules, namely the modules
L_{\mu}
such that
\mu=w\cdot\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
for some
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

We define a
block
in
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
by separating indecomposable modules which are homologically unrelated via the Ext functor. We write
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
to denote the block containing
L_{\lambda}
. The block
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
is a subcategory of
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})_{\lambda}
, and furthermore, Enright and Shelton
[
13
,
14
]
showed that each
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})_{\lambda}
decomposes into at most two blocks. For a block
\mathcal{B}
, we define the
poset of
\mathcal{B}
to be

\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\coloneqq\{\mu\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})\mid L_{\mu}\text{ is an object in $\mathcal{B}$}\},

via the usual ordering on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. From now on, we reserve the symbol
\lambda
to denote a
quasidominant weight
: that is to say,
\lambda
is the unique maximal element of
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
. We write
\mu
when referring to an arbitrary element of
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
. We say that
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
is
regular
if
\langle\lambda+\rho,\>\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\neq 0
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. If
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
is regular, then
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})_{\lambda}=\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
is itself a block, called a
regular block
; in this case,
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\cong\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
as posets.

In order to capture three of the four important aspects of the situation in Example
1.1
, we follow
[
1
]
*Def. 3.4.1 in defining the following notion of congruent blocks:

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
and
(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
be Hermitian symmetric pairs. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a block in
\mathcal{O}\coloneqq\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
a block in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
. We say that
\mathcal{B}
is
congruent
to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
if

(1)

we have an isomorphism of posets
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})\cong\Lambda(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
, where we write
\mu\mapsto\mu^{\prime}
;

(2)

for all
\mu,\nu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
where
\mu<\nu
, and for all
i\geq 0
, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{O}}(N_{\mu},L_{\nu})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(N_{\mu^{\prime}},L_{\nu^{\prime}})
;

(3)

for all
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
, we have
\dim F_{\mu}=\dim F_{\mu^{\prime}}
.

If
\lambda
is the maximal element in the poset
\Lambda(\mathcal{B})
, then properties (1) and (2) above guarantee that
L_{\lambda}
is a Kostant module (in the language of
[
12
]
). Therefore
L_{\lambda}
has a BGG resolution
[
11
]
*Thm. 2.8, even though (as in the singular case described in the following subsection)
L_{\lambda}
may not be a finite-dimensional module.

Revisiting Example
1.1
in light of Definition
5.1
, we can now say that the regular block
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{D}_{4},\mathsf{A}_{3})_{0}
is congruent to the regular block
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{C}_{3},\mathsf{A}_{2})_{0}
.

5.3.
Enright–Shelton reduction

Recall that whenever
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
is regular, we have
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})_{\lambda}=\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\cong\prescript{\mathfrak{k}}{}{\mathcal{W}}
as posets. If, on the other hand,
\langle\lambda+\rho,\>\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=0
for some
\alpha\in\Phi
, then we say that
\lambda
is
singular
, and
\alpha
is a
singular root
with respect to
\lambda
. We also say that
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
is a
singular block
. (For all six families in Table
2
, note that
\lambda
is singular.) Loosely speaking, the process of
Enright–Shelton reduction
converts a singular weight (plus
\rho
) in
\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
into a regular weight (plus
\rho^{\prime}
) in
\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
, where
(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
is a certain Hermitian symmetric pair whose rank is less than that of
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
.
In the following discussion, we will explain the details of this reduction for the specific instances of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
listed in Table
2
. We will write a superscript
\flat
to denote the result of Enright–Shelton reduction:

\mu+\rho\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt{\rm ES}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mu+\rho)^{\flat}=\mu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},

which induces the map

(5.1)

\mu\longmapsto\mu^{\prime}=(\mu+\rho)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}.

The reduction is invertible, and we will write a superscript
\sharp
to denote its inverse:

\mu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt{\rm ES}^{-1}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}=\mu+\rho,

which induces the inverse map of (
5.1
), namely

(5.2)

\mu^{\prime}\longmapsto\mu=(\mu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-\rho.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
13
,
14
]
)
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
be a Hermitian symmetric pair. Let
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k})
with
L_{\lambda}\neq N_{\lambda}
, and let
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\!+\!}(\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
be the regular weight obtained from
\lambda
by Enright–Shelton reduction. Then the map (
5.1
) induced by Enright–Shelton reduction restricts to an isomorphism of posets

\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})

satisfying conditions (1) and (2) of Definition (
5.1
).

Type

\mathfrak{g}

\lambda

elements
\pi^{*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})

m

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}

\lambda^{\prime}

\pi^{\prime*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})

[2pt]
I

\mathsf{A}_{P+Q-1}

-k\omega_{P}

(\alpha\mid\beta+m)^{*}

\otimes

(\beta\mid\alpha+m)
,

(\alpha|\beta)\in\Par(p\times q)

k

\mathsf{A}_{p+q-1}

k\omega^{\prime}_{p}

(\alpha+m\mid\beta)^{*}

\otimes

(\beta+m\mid\alpha)
,

(\alpha|\beta)\in\Par(p\times q)

[2pt]
II

\mathsf{D}_{N}

-2k\omega_{N}

(\alpha\mid\alpha+m)^{*}

2k+1

\mathsf{C}_{n}

k\omega^{\prime}_{n}

(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)^{*}

[2pt]
IIIa

\mathsf{C}_{N}

-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}

(\alpha\mid\alpha+m)^{*}

even rank

k-1

\mathsf{D}_{n}

even rank

[2pt]

even rank

IIId

\mathsf{D}_{N}

-(2k-1)\omega_{N}+\omega^{*}_{2k+1}

2k
