(\alpha\mid\alpha+m)^{*}

odd rank

Table 2.
Congruence of singular and regular blocks
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. (See Theorem
5.5
.) On the right side of the table, we put
p=P-m
,
q=Q-m
, and
n=N-m
. To avoid trivial cases, we let
1\leq k\leq\text{rank of $(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})$}
, although Type II remains of interest for
k=0
. We write
\omega^{*}_{k}
merely to denote the
N
-tuple whose last
k
coordinates are
-1
, with
0
elsewhere. We write
\omega^{\prime}_{i}
for the fundamental weights of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.

We now detail the process of Enright–Shelton reduction for each of the six families in Table
2
. Note that we label each family with respect to
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
rather than
\mathfrak{g}
, reflecting our philosophy that the regular blocks are easier to understand than the singular blocks. We use capital letters
P
,
Q
, and
N
to describe the rank of
\mathfrak{g}
; then defining
m
to be the number of coordinates deleted via reduction (details below), we describe the rank of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
using the lower-case letters
p\coloneqq P-m
,
q\coloneqq Q-m
, and
n\coloneqq N-m
. In each family, the parameter
k
ranges over all positive integers strictly less than the rank of
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
, which is
\min\{P,Q\}
,
N
, or
\lfloor N/2\rfloor
, depending on whether
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{A}_{P+Q-1}
,
\mathsf{C}_{N}
, or
\mathsf{D}_{N}
, respectively.

Type I
(
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{A}_{P+Q-1}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{A}_{p+q-1}
;
\lambda=-k\omega_{P}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{p}
). Suppose
\mu+\rho=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{P};b_{1},\ldots,b_{Q})
. Then
\mu+\rho
contains a singularity for each instance of an equality
a_{i}=b_{j}
, in which case a singular root is
\beta_{ij}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{P+j}
. Hence to perform the reduction on
\mu+\rho
, we delete all coordinate pairs
a_{i},b_{j}
such that
a_{i}=b_{j}
. Then
m
is the number of such pairs.

For example, let
P=4
and
Q=3
, with
k=2
. Then
\lambda=(-2,-2,-2,-2;0,0,0)
, and
\lambda+\rho=(4,3,2,1;2,1,0)
. Therefore by (
5.1
), we have

\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(4,3,\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1};\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1},0)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(4,3;0)-(2,1;0)=(2,2;0)=2\omega^{\prime}_{2}.

Note that the reduction deletes
m=2
pairs of coordinates, so that
p=2
,
q=1
, and indeed
\lambda^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{p}
. As an example of an arbitrary element of
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
, we choose
\mu=(-2,-3,-3,-3;1,1,1)
. Then
\mu+\rho=(4,2,1,0;3,2,1)
, and we have

\mu^{\prime}=(4,\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1},0;3,\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1})^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(4,0;3)-(2,1;0)=(2,-1;3).

Type II
(
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{N}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{C}_{n}
;
\lambda=-2k\omega_{N}
,
\lambda=k\omega^{\prime}_{n}
). This case is exceptional among the six families in Table
2
, because the
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
obtained by Enright–Shelton reduction is actually
\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
, not
\mathsf{C}_{n}
. In order to obtain the result in Table
2
, we compose Enright–Shelton reduction with a further reduction from
\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
to
\mathsf{C}_{n}
, by deleting the 0 from
(\mu+\rho)^{\flat}
. This second reduction also satisfies conditions (1) and (2) in Definition
5.1
. The reason for our modification here is this: for the other five families, it turns out that Enright–Shelton reduction satisfies condition (3) as well, producing congruent blocks, but in Type II, the extra reduction is necessary to fulfill condition (3).

Suppose
\mu+\rho=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
, where the
a_{i}
are integers. Then
\mu+\rho
contains a singularity for each instance of an equality
a_{i}=-a_{j}<0
, in which case a singular root is
\beta_{ij}=\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}
. To perform the reduction on
\mu+\rho
, we delete all coordinate pairs
a_{i},a_{j}
such that
a_{i}=-a_{j}
. Then as mentioned above, we perform a second reduction by deleting the coordinate 0.

For example, let
N=8
, with
k=2
. Then
\lambda=(-2,\ldots,-2)
, and
\lambda+\rho=(5,4,\ldots,-1,-2)
. Therefore, we have

\lambda+\rho\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt{\rm ES}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=(5,4,3,\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1},0,\mathbf{-1},\mathbf{-2})^{\flat}=(5,4,3,0)\xrightarrow{\text{delete $0$}}(5,4,3)

as a weight in Type
\mathsf{C}_{3}
. Then, treating
(5,4,3)
as the term
(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}
in (
5.1
), we have

\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(5,4,3)-(3,2,1)=(2,2,2)=2\omega_{3}.

Ultimately we have deleted
m=5
coordinates. As an example of an arbitrary element of
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
, we take
\mu=(-2,-4,-4,-4,-4,-4,-4)
. Then
\mu+\rho=(5,2,1,0,-1,-2,-3,-4)
, and we have

(5,\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1},0,\mathbf{-1},\mathbf{-2},-3,-4)^{\flat}=(5,0,-3,-4)\xrightarrow{\text{delete $0$}}(5,-3,-4),

and so

\mu^{\prime}=(5,-3,-4)-(3,2,1)=(2,-5,-5).

(We will revisit this setting in Example
5.7
.)

Type IIIa
(
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{C}_{N}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
;
\lambda=-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{n}
). Suppose
\mu+\rho=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
with the
a_{i}
either all integers or all half-integers. Then
\mu+\rho
contains a singularity for each instance of an equality
a_{i}=-a_{j}<0
, in which case a singular root is
\beta_{ij}
; moreover, if
k
is even, then
\mu+\rho
contains another singularity at the coordinate
a_{i}=0
, which means that we have a (long) singular root
\beta_{ii}=2\varepsilon_{i}
. To perform the reduction on
\mu+\rho
, we delete all pairs of opposite coordinates, along with 0 (if applicable).

For example, let
N=7
and
k=4
. Then
\lambda=(-2,\ldots,-2)
, and
\lambda+\rho=(5,4,3,2,1,0,-1)
. Then we reduce by deleting
m=3
coordinates, and we have

\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(5,4,3,2,\mathbf{1},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{-1})^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(5,4,3,2)-(3,2,1,0)=(2,2,2,2)=4\omega^{\prime}_{4}.

On the other hand, if
k=5
, then
\lambda=\left(-\frac{5}{2},\ldots,-\frac{5}{2}\right)
and
\lambda+\rho=\left(\frac{9}{2},\frac{7}{2},\ldots,-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{3}{2}\right)
. We now delete
m=4
coordinates, and obtain

\lambda^{\prime}=\left(\frac{9}{2},\frac{7}{2},\frac{5}{2},\mathbf{\frac{3}{2}},\mathbf{\frac{1}{2}},\mathbf{-\frac{1}{2}},\mathbf{-\frac{3}{2}}\right)^{\!\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=\left(\frac{9}{2},\frac{7}{2},\frac{5}{2}\right)-(2,1,0)=\left(\frac{5}{2},\frac{5}{2},\frac{5}{2}\right)=5\omega^{\prime}_{3}.

Type IIIb
(
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{C}_{N}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
;
\lambda=-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}+\omega^{*}_{k}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}
). The reduction procedure is the same as in Type IIIa.
As an example, let
N=7
and
k=4
. Then
\lambda=(-2,-2,-2,-3,-3,-3,-3)
, and
\lambda+\rho=(5,4,3,1,0,-1,-2)
. We have

\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(5,4,3,\mathbf{1},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{-1},-2)^{\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=(5,4,3,-2)-(3,2,1,0)=(2,2,2,-2)=4\omega^{\prime}_{3}.

When
k
is odd, the half-integral case works out similarly.

Type IIIc
(
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{N}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
;
\lambda=-(2k-1)\omega_{N}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n}
). The reduction procedure is the same as in Type II, and there is no need for the extra reduction step since in this case we always obtain half-integer coordinates. As an example, let
N=4
and
k=1
. Then
\lambda=\left(-\frac{1}{2},\ldots,-\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\lambda+\rho=\left(\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)
. We reduce by deleting
m=2
coordinates, and we have

\lambda^{\prime}=\left(\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2},\mathbf{\frac{1}{2}},\mathbf{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{\!\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=\left(\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)-(1,0)=\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)=3\omega^{\prime}_{2}.

Type IIId
(
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{N}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
;
\lambda=-(2k-1)\omega_{N}+\omega^{*}_{2k+1}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}
). The reduction procedure is the same as in Type IIIc. As an example, let
N=6
and
k=2
. Then
\lambda=\left(-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{2},-\frac{5}{2},-\frac{5}{2},-\frac{5}{2},-\frac{5}{2}\right)
and
\lambda+\rho=\left(\frac{7}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{3}{2},-\frac{5}{2}\right)
. We reduce by deleting
m=4
coordinates, and we have

\lambda^{\prime}=\left(\frac{7}{2},\mathbf{\frac{3}{2}},\mathbf{\frac{1}{2}},\mathbf{-\frac{1}{2}},\mathbf{-\frac{3}{2}},-\frac{5}{2}\right)^{\!\flat}-\rho^{\prime}=\left(\frac{7}{2},-\frac{5}{2}\right)-(1,0)=\left(\frac{5}{2},\frac{5}{2}\right)=5\omega^{\prime}_{1}.

5.4.
Twisted posets of the regular blocks

In order to see how conjugate partitions are related to congruent blocks, we introduce a “twist” to the posets
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
and
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
. Let
\zeta
be the unique fundamental weight of
\mathfrak{g}
that is orthogonal to
\Phi(\mathfrak{k})
; likewise, let
\zeta^{\prime}
be the unique fundamental weight of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
orthogonal to
\Phi(\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
. Let
\beta
be the highest root of
\mathfrak{g}
, and
\beta^{\prime}
the highest root of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
. We note that
\langle\zeta,\beta^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\zeta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle=1
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
belong to one of the families in Table
2
. We define the
twisted posets

\displaystyle\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\mu-\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta\mid\mu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\},

\displaystyle\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\mu^{\prime}-\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}\mid\mu^{\prime}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})\}.

Note that, except in Types IIIb and IIId, the weight
\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta
is the same as
\lambda
, and
\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}
is the same as
\lambda^{\prime}
. Hence outside Types IIIb and IIIc, the twisted posets have the weight 0 as their maximal element. For Types IIIb and IIIc,
\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta
is just the
\lambda
from Types IIIa and IIIc, respectively; the same is true for
\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}
and the
\lambda^{\prime}
. Clearly
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\cong\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
and
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})\cong\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
as posets, and any poset map
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
induces a unique map
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
between twisted posets. In the context of BGG resolutions, the twist amounts to tensoring with the
1
-dimensional
\mathfrak{k}
-module
F_{-\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta}
or the
1
-dimensional
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
-module
F_{-\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we will write

(5.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}&\coloneqq L_{\lambda}\otimes F_{-\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta,}\\
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}&\coloneqq L_{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes F_{-\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}.}\end{split}

In the following lemma, we establish the final column of Table
2
, which is an explicit description of the elements of
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
. The flow of our argument in each type is this: we use Enright–Shelton reduction on
\lambda
to obtain
\lambda^{\prime}
, which determines the filling of diagrams
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. From this filling we show that
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda}
is the result of uniformly lengthening the arms or legs of
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
, whose shape we already understand from (
4.10
) and (
4.11
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
,
\lambda
, and
\lambda^{\prime}
belong to one of the six families in Table
2
. Then
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
is the set of all weights
\pi^{\prime*}
shown in the last column of Table
2
.

Proof.

Type I
(
\lambda=-k\omega_{P}
). We have
\lambda+\rho=(P+Q-1-k,\ldots,Q-k;\>Q-1,\ldots,0)
, where the ellipses denote coordinates decreasing by 1. Thus the
(P+1-k)
th coordinate is
Q-1
, meaning that the string of
k
coordinates before the semicolon equals the string of
k
coordinates after the semicolon. Hence in the Enright–Shelton reduction, we delete these
m=k
pairs of coordinates, so that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{A}_{P+Q-2k-1}=\mathsf{A}_{p+q-1}
and
\rho^{\prime}=(p+q-1,\ldots,0)
.
Thus

\displaystyle(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(p+q+k-1,\ldots,q+k;q-1,\ldots,0),

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(k,\ldots,k;0,\ldots,0)=k\omega^{\prime}_{p}.

It is clear that
d_{i}\coloneqq\langle\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\>\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle=1
for all
i\neq p
, while
d_{p}=k+1
. Hence every non-diagonal entry in
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is 1, while every diagonal entry is
k+1
. Therefore
\cols\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the result of adding
k
to each leg of
[\Phi_{w}]
and then taking the conjugate; likewise,
\rows\,[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the result of adding
k
to each arm of
[\Phi_{w}]
. Therefore, setting
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|\beta)
, and recalling that
m=k
, we use (
4.12
) to obtain

\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}=((\beta+m\mid\alpha)^{*};\>(\alpha+m\mid\beta)).

By Theorem
4.5
and Definition (
5.3
), each element of
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
equals
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}
for a unique
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}
. We therefore have

\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})=\Big\{(\alpha+m\mid\beta)^{*}\otimes(\beta+m\mid\alpha)\>\Big|\>(\alpha|\beta)\in\Par(p\times q)\Big\},

as desired.

Type II
(
\lambda=-2k\omega_{N}
). We have
\lambda+\rho=(N-k-1,\ldots,-k)
, where the ellipsis denotes coordinates decreasing by 1. Thus the final
2k+1
coordinates are the string
(k,\ldots,-k)
, all of which (except the 0) is deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction. We then have
(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=(N-k-1,\ldots,k+1,0)
. After deleting the
0
(as described in Section
5.3
), we have deleted
m=2k+1
coordinates, so that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{C}_{N-2k-1}=\mathsf{C}_{n}
and
\rho^{\prime}=(n,\ldots,1)
. Thus

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(k+n,\ldots,k+1),

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(k,\ldots,k)=k\omega^{\prime}_{n}.

It is clear that
d_{i}=1
for all
i\neq n
, while
d_{n}=k+1
. Hence every non-diagonal entry in
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is
1
, while every diagonal entry is
k+1
. Therefore
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the result of adding
2k
to each arm of
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
. But if
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|0)
, then by (
4.11
) we have
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)
, and so
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}=(\alpha+2k+1\mid\alpha)=(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
. By Theorem
4.5
and Definition (
5.3
), each element of
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
equals
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}
for a unique
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}
, and hence
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})=\{(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)^{*}\mid\alpha_{1}<n\}
, as desired.

Type IIIa
(
\lambda=-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}
). We have
\lambda+\rho=(N-\frac{k}{2},\ldots,1-\frac{k}{2})
, where the ellipsis denotes coordinates decreasing by 1. Thus the
(k-1)
th coordinate from the end equals
k-1-\frac{k}{2}=\frac{k}{2}-1
, and so the final
k-1
coordinates are the string
(\frac{k}{2}-1,\ldots,1-\frac{k}{2})
, which is deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction. Hence
m=k-1
, so that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{N-k+1}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
and
\rho^{\prime}=(n-1,\ldots,0)
. Thus

\displaystyle(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(n-1+\frac{k}{2},\ldots,\frac{k}{2}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2},\ldots,\frac{k}{2}\right)=k\omega^{\prime}_{n}.

We therefore have
d_{i}=1
for all
i\neq n
, while
d_{n}=k+1
. Hence all entries in
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
are
1
, except for the odd diagonal entries, which are
k+1
. Therefore in
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, the entries
k+1
occur in consecutive vertical pairs : explicitly, in positions
(2i-1,\>2i-1)
and
(2i,\>2i-1)
, for
i=1,\ldots,h\coloneqq\lceil\rk[\Phi_{w}]/2\rceil
. It follows that
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the result of adding
k
to the first
h
row pairs in
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
, which forces
\rk\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}=2h
. If
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha|0)
, then by (
4.11
) we have
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket=(\alpha\mid\alpha+1)
, and so
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}=(\alpha+k\mid\alpha+1)
, where if
\rk\alpha
is odd then we augment
\alpha
by inserting
(\ldots,-1\mid\ldots,0)
. By allowing
\alpha_{1}<n
rather than
\alpha_{1}<n-1
, we can rewrite partitions of this form as
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}=(\alpha+k-1\mid\alpha)=(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
with even rank. The rest follows as in the previous cases.

Type IIIb
(
\lambda=-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}+\omega_{k}^{*}
).
We have

\lambda+\rho=\Big(\underbrace{N-\tfrac{k}{2},\ldots,\tfrac{k}{2}+1}_{N-k},\underbrace{\tfrac{k}{2}-1,\ldots,-\tfrac{k}{2}}_{k}\Big),

where the ellipses denote coordinates decreasing by 1. Thus the final
k
coordinates, except for the very last one, are all deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction. Hence
m=k-1
, so that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{N-k+1}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
and
\rho^{\prime}=(n-1,\ldots,0)
. Thus

\displaystyle(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(n-1+\frac{k}{2},\ldots,\frac{k}{2}+1,-\frac{k}{2}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2},\ldots,\frac{k}{2},-\frac{k}{2}\right)=k\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}.

We therefore have
d_{i}=1
for all
i\neq n-1
, while
d_{n-1}=k+1
. Hence
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the same as in Type IIIa, except that
k+1
occurs as the
even
diagonal entries. As a result,
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
has
odd
rank, but is not in general a true partition since its first arm is not longer than its second arm: indeed, it is obtained from
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
by adding
k
to the first
\lfloor\rk[\Phi_{w}]/2\rfloor
row pairs
beneath
the first row.
Recall, however, that in Type IIIb, we define
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
in terms of the
\lambda^{\prime}
from Type IIIa, namely
k\omega^{\prime}_{n}
; since the difference of the two
\lambda^{\prime}
s is
k\varepsilon_{1}^{*}
, we have

\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})=\left\{(k\varepsilon_{1}+\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}})^{*}\>\middle|\>w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}\right\}.

This addition of
k\varepsilon_{1}
adds back the “missing”
k
to the first arm of
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, so that elements of
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
are the duals of the partitions
(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
with odd rank, where
\alpha_{1}<n
.

Type IIIc
(
\lambda=-(2k-1)\omega_{N}
). We have
\lambda+\rho=(N-k-\frac{1}{2},\ldots,-k+\frac{1}{2})
, where the ellipsis denotes coordinates decreasing by 1. Thus the final
2k
coordinates are the string
(k-\frac{1}{2},\ldots,-k+\frac{1}{2})
, which is deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction. Hence
m=2k
, so that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{N-2k}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
and
\rho^{\prime}=(n-1,\ldots,0)
. Thus

\displaystyle(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(n+k-\frac{1}{2},\ldots,k+\frac{1}{2}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2k+1}{2},\ldots,\frac{2k+1}{2}\right)=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n}.

The rest of the argument is identical to Type IIIa, where
m=2k
instead of
m=k-1
.

Type IIId
(
\lambda=-(2k-1)\omega_{N}+\omega_{2k+1}^{*}
).
We have

\lambda+\rho=\Big(\underbrace{N-k-\tfrac{1}{2},\ldots,k+\tfrac{3}{2}}_{N-2k-1},\underbrace{k-\tfrac{1}{2},\ldots,-k-\tfrac{1}{2}}_{2k+1}\Big),

where the ellipses denote coordinates decreasing by 1. Thus the final
2k+1
coordinates, except for the very last one, are all deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction. Hence
m=2k
, so that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{N-2k}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
and
\rho^{\prime}=(n-1,\ldots,0)
. Thus

\displaystyle(\lambda+\rho)^{\flat}=\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(n+k-\frac{1}{2},\ldots,k+\frac{3}{2},-k-\frac{1}{2}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2k+1}{2},\ldots,\frac{2k+1}{2},-\frac{2k+1}{2}\right)=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}.

The rest of the argument is identical to Type IIIb, where
m=2k
instead of
m=k-1
.
∎

5.5.
Main result: congruent blocks and conjugate partitions

We arrive at our main result:

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
, along with
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
, belong to one of the six families in Table
2
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
is congruent to
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. Moreover, we have an isomorphism of twisted posets

\displaystyle\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle\pi^{*}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\pi^{\prime*}

such that
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are conjugate partitions.

Remark 5.6
.

In Type I, the weight
\pi^{\prime}=\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is not a true partition, but rather a pair of partitions, where the dual operation
(\>)^{*}
has been applied to the first partition.
Hence
\pi^{\prime}
is represented by a corner-to-corner stacking of the two Young diagrams, where the first is rotated 180 degrees so that its row lengths are considered negative. (Recall the stacked diagrams
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
in Type I, which took the same form.) Seeing as how such stacked diagrams resemble a butterfly, we will refer to each of the two diagrams as a “wing” of
\pi^{\prime}
.
Likewise,
\pi
is represented by a butterfly diagram. The claim in the theorem is that
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
are conjugates, i.e., we can obtain one from the other by reflecting about the 45-degree axis through the center of the butterfly.

From now on, thanks to Lemma
5.4
, we will write
\pi^{\prime*}
for an arbitrary element of
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
, where
\pi^{\prime}
is a partition (except in Type I, where
\pi
contains
q
negative coordinates followed by
p
positive coordinates). By Theorem
5.2
, there is a poset isomorphism
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
induced by Enright–Shelton reduction, which further induces a poset isomorphism
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
. We denote the preimage of
\pi^{\prime*}
by writing
\pi^{*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
, without making any assumptions about the nature of
\pi=(\pi^{*})^{*}
itself. Explicitly, we must have

\pi=[(\pi^{\prime*}+\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-(\lambda+\rho)]^{*}.

(As before, in Types IIIb and IIId we must use the
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
from Types IIIa and IIIc, respectively.) Since
\pi^{\prime*}=w\cdot\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}
for some
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}
, the claim in Theorem
5.5
is that the two weights

(5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi^{\prime}&=[w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})-(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})]^{*},\\
\pi&=[w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-(\lambda+\rho)]^{*}\end{split}

are truly conjugate partitions for all
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}
. Before proving Theorem
5.5
, we present a detailed example that illuminates the way in which these conjugate partitions arise. Our approach is to begin with
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
, which we understand completely thanks to Lemma
4.1
, and then reverse the Enright–Shelton reduction to pass to
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
. This philosophy — taking the regular block as our starting point in order to understand the singular block — is the reason for our labeling the various types in terms of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
rather than
\mathfrak{g}
.

(5,4,3,\blacksquare)
(5,4,\blacksquare,\overline{3})
(5,3,\blacksquare,\overline{4})
(4,3,\blacksquare,\overline{5})
(5,\blacksquare,\overline{3},\overline{4})
(4,\blacksquare,\overline{3},\overline{5})
(3,\blacksquare,\overline{4},\overline{5})
(\blacksquare,\overline{3},\overline{4},\overline{5})

Without
\blacksquare
:

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})

With
\blacksquare
:

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}

[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}

Figure 4.
Illustration of Example
5.7
. The symbol
\blacksquare
denotes the string
(2,1,0,\overline{1},\overline{2})
deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction (and subsequent deletion of 0). For typographical clarity, the bars denote negatives.

Example 5.7
.

We revisit our previous example in Type II, where
N=8
and
k=2
. (See the case-by-case descriptions at the end of Section
5.3
.) For typographical clarity, we write negatives as bars over the coordinates; hence we have
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{8}
with
\lambda=(\overline{2},\ldots,\overline{2})
, and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{C}_{3}
with
\lambda^{\prime}=(2,2,2)
. Thus
\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}=(5,4,3)
.

We refer the reader to Figure
4
throughout this example. On the right, we represent
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}
by the diagram
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. On the left, we simultaneously depict
both
posets
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
and
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
. The symbol
\blacksquare\coloneqq(2,1,0,\overline{1},\overline{2})
denotes the string of
m=5
coordinates deleted via Enright–Shelton reduction (and subsequent deletion of the 0). In this way, ignoring the
\blacksquare
gives us
w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
, while retaining the
\blacksquare
gives us
w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}
. (We abuse notation slightly by writing
\sharp
to reverse both the 0-deletion and the Enright–Shelton reduction.) Upon subtracting either
\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}
or
\lambda+\rho
, we have the elements of either
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
or
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
.

Our goal is to understand why (the duals of) corresponding elements
\pi^{\prime*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
and
\pi^{*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
are conjugate partitions. In order to compare inductively the construction of
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
, it suffices to consider the general case where
w
covers
v
, i.e., where
[\Phi_{v}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is joined from above to
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
in Figure
4
. In other words, we consider the effect of adding one box to
[\Phi_{v}]
to obtain
[\Phi_{w}]
. In the base case, at the top of Figure
4
where
w={\rm id}
, we have from (
5.4
) that
\pi^{\prime}=\pi=0
. Therefore, again by (
5.4
), it actually suffices to compare the differences

(5.5)

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})\qquad\text{and}\qquad w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp},

which (upon taking the dual) will tell us how
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
are constructed as
[\Phi_{w}]
is built box by box.

We adopt the convention of building
[\Phi_{w}]
by rows from top to bottom, adding boxes in a given row from left to right. Hence for our purposes, there are at most two ways to add a box to
[\Phi_{v}]
: either add a new box along the diagonal, or add a box to the bottom row.

Case 1: adding a diagonal box.

Suppose that
[\Phi_{w}]
is obtained by adding the
i
th diagonal box to
[\Phi_{v}]
, which corresponds to the root
2\varepsilon_{n+1-i}
. It follows from Lemma
4.1
that
w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
is obtained by subtracting
2(k+1)=6
from the
i
th coordinate of
v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
, counting from the right. We claim (but we save for the actual proof) that this coordinate is necessarily
k+1=3
, which therefore is negated by our subtraction. In Figure
4
, we thus have

(5.6)

(\ldots,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$th from the right,}\\
\text{ignoring the $\blacksquare$}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{3},\blacksquare,\ldots)\quad\leadsto\quad(\ldots,\blacksquare,\overline{3},\ldots).

Therefore, on one hand, ignoring the
\blacksquare
in (
5.6
) gives us

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})=(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th from the right}\end{subarray}}{\overline{6}},0,\ldots,0),

which (upon taking the dual) creates the
i
th arm in
\pi^{\prime}
, with length
m=6-1=5
. On the other hand, including the
\blacksquare
in (
5.6
) gives us

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}=(0,\ldots,0,\underbrace{\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{1}}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$m+1$ coordinates},\\
\text{ending $i$th from the right}\end{subarray}}},0,\ldots,0),

which (upon taking the dual) creates the
i
th leg in
\pi
, with length
m=5
. (This fact requires that each previous leg must be strictly longer than
m
, which will be clear from Case 2 below.) Hence the case of adding a diagonal box preserves the conjugate shapes of
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
.

As a specific example of Case 1, in Figure
4
we choose
[\Phi_{v}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}*(gray!30)\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 3$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
, and add a diagonal box to begin row
i=2
, thus obtaining
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
to the southeast. On the left side, we thus have

(5,3,\blacksquare,\overline{4})\quad\leadsto\quad(5,\blacksquare,\overline{3},\overline{4}).

We confirm that this has the effect of negating the
3
, which was indeed in position
i=2
from the right. Moreover, we have the differences
(5,\overline{3},\overline{4})-(5,3,\overline{4})=(0,\overline{6},0)
and
(5,\blacksquare,\overline{3},\overline{4})-(5,3,\blacksquare,\overline{4})=(0,\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},0)
.

Case 2: adding a non-diagonal box.
Suppose that
[\Phi_{w}]
is obtained by adding the
j
th non-diagonal box to the
i
th row of
[\Phi_{v}]
, which we assume is the bottom row. This box corresponds to the root
\varepsilon_{n+1-i}+\varepsilon_{n+1-i-j}
. It follows from Lemma
4.1
that
w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
is obtained by subtracting
1
from the
i
th and
(i+j)
th coordinates of
v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
, counting from the right. We claim (but we reserve for the proof) that these coordinates are necessarily
\overline{j+k}
and
j+k+1
, which are therefore transposed and negated by our subtraction. In our current example where
k=2
, these two coordinates are
\overline{j+2}
and
j+3
. In Figure
4
, we thus have

(5.7)

(\ldots,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(i+j)$th}\\
\text{from the right,}\\
\text{ignoring the $\blacksquare$}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{j+3},\ldots\ldots,\blacksquare,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$th}\\
\text{from the right}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{\overline{j+2}},\ldots)\quad\leadsto\quad(\ldots,j+2,\ldots\ldots,\blacksquare,\overline{j+3},\ldots).

Therefore, on one hand, ignoring the
\blacksquare
in (
5.7
) gives us

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})=(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(i+j)$th}\\
\text{from right}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{\overline{1}},0,\ldots\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$th}\\
\text{from right}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{\overline{1}},0,\ldots,0),

which (upon taking the dual) adds a box to the
i
th arm and the
i
th leg of
\pi^{\prime}
. (Note that each previous leg of
\pi
must be strictly longer than
j
, by the very fact that we are able to add the
j
th box to the bottom row of
[\Phi_{v}]
.) On the other hand, including the
\blacksquare
in (
5.7
) gives us

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}=(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(m+i+j)$th}\\
\text{from right}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{\overline{1}},0,\ldots\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$th}\\
\text{from right}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{\overline{1}},0,\ldots,0),

which (upon taking the dual) also adds a box to the
i
th arm and the
i
th leg of
\pi
. Hence the case of adding a non-diagonal box preserves the conjugate shapes of
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
.

As a specific example of Case 2, we again choose
[\Phi_{v}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}*(gray!30)\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 3$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
, but this time we add box
j=2
to the bottom (i.e., only) row
i=1
to obtain
[\Phi_{w}]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
to its southwest. On the left side, we thus have

(5,3,\blacksquare,\overline{4})\quad\leadsto\quad(4,3,\blacksquare,\overline{5}).

We confirm that this has the effect of subtracting
1
from the coordinates
\overline{j+2}=\overline{4}
and
j+3=5
, which were in positions
i=1
and
i+j=3
, counting from the right. Moreover, we have the differences
(4,3,\overline{5})-(5,3,\overline{4})=(\overline{1},0,\overline{1})
and
(4,3,\blacksquare,\overline{5})-(5,3,\blacksquare,\overline{4})=(\overline{1},0,0,0,0,0,0,\overline{1})
.

Having concluded our preliminary example in Type II, we proceed to prove Theorem
5.5
. The proof, for each of the types, merely makes rigorous the same idea that drives Example
5.7
; for this reason, we encourage the reader to begin reading the proof for Type II before the other types.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

Once we have shown for each type that
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
are conjugate partitions, the congruence of blocks
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
will follow immediately from Lemma
5.4
and Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
. Hence we prove the conjugate property for each type:

Type I.
As for Type II below, formally the proof relies on the following analogue of (
5.9
): if
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha-1\mid\beta-1)
has rank
r
, then we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}w(c_{p},\ldots,c_{1}&;d_{1},\ldots,d_{q})\\
=&(c_{p},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\beta_{1}}},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\beta_{r}}},\ldots,c_{1},d_{\alpha_{r}},\ldots,d_{\alpha_{1}};c_{\beta_{1}},\ldots,c_{\beta_{r}},d_{1},\ldots,\widehat{d_{\alpha_{r}}},\ldots,\widehat{d_{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,d_{q}),\end{split}

where the hats denote missing coordinates. This can be shown inductively using the same method as in (
5.9
).

Recall from Lemma
5.4
that
\lambda+\rho=(p+q+k-1,\ldots,q+k,\blacksquare;\blacksquare,q-1,\ldots,0)
, where
\blacksquare=(q+k-1,\ldots,q)
is the string deleted on either side of the semicolon via Enright–Shelton reduction. Therefore
\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}=(p+q+k-1,\ldots,q+k;q-1,\ldots,0)
. By (
5.4
), in the base case where
w={\rm id}
we have
\pi^{\prime}=\pi=0
, and so just as in Example
5.7
, it suffices to consider the differences (
5.5
) when
[\Phi_{w}]
is obtained by adding a single box to
[\Phi_{v}]
.

First suppose we obtain
[\Phi_{w}]
by adding the
i
th diagonal box to
[\Phi_{v}]
, which corresponds to the root
\varepsilon_{p+1-i}-\varepsilon_{p+i}
. Then by Lemma
4.1
,
w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
is obtained from
v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
by subtracting
k+1
from the
i
th coordinate to the left of the semicolon, and adding
k+1
to the
i
th coordinate to the right of the semicolon.
1
1

1

Of course, we could apply (
4.3
) rather than Lemma
4.1
, and thus simply transpose the two
i
th coordinates in both directions from the semicolon; the reader can check that this has the exact same effect because of our particular
\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}
. For the sake of uniformity, however, we have decided to use the method in Lemma
5.4
throughout this proof.
It follows from (
5.8
) that these coordinates are
q+k
on the left, and
q-1
on the right, and hence we have

(\ldots,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th to the left}\end{subarray}}{q+k},\blacksquare,\ldots;\ldots,\blacksquare,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th to the right}\end{subarray}}{q-1},\ldots)\quad\leadsto\quad(\ldots,\blacksquare,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th to the left}\end{subarray}}{q-1},\ldots;\ldots,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th to the right}\end{subarray}}{q+k},\blacksquare,\ldots),

counting coordinates from the semicolon, without counting the
\blacksquare
. On one hand, ignoring the
\blacksquare
gives us

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})=(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th to the left}\end{subarray}}{\overline{k+1}},0,\ldots,0;0,\ldots,0,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$i$th to the right}\end{subarray}}{k+1},0,\ldots,0),

which (upon taking the dual) creates the
i
th leg of length
m=k
in each of the wings of
\pi^{\prime}
. (Recall Remark
5.6
on the butterfly diagrams that represent
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
.) On the other hand, including the
\blacksquare
gives us

w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}-v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\sharp}=(0,\ldots,0,\underbrace{\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{1}}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$m+1$ coordinates,}\\
\text{ending $i$th to the left}\end{subarray}}},0,\ldots,0;0,\ldots,0,\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$m+1$ coordinates,}\\
\text{starting $i$th to the right}\end{subarray}}},0,\ldots,0),

which (upon taking the dual) creates the
i
th
leg
of length
m
in each of the wings of
\pi
.

The remaining possibilities work out similarly, following from Lemma
4.1
and (
5.8
). In sum, adding a box to the
i
th arm of
[\Phi_{v}]
adds a box to the
i
th arm of each wing of
\pi^{\prime}
, but adds a box to the
i
th
leg
of each wing of
\pi
; the same remains true if we interchange the words “arm” and “leg” everywhere. Hence
\pi
is obtained from
\pi^{\prime}
by a reflection about the 45-degree axis, and so the two are conjugates.

Type II.
Our only task is to verify the two inductive claims made in Example
5.7
. In Case 1, we claimed that whenever it is possible to add the
i
th diagonal box to
[\Phi_{v}]
, the weight
v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
must have
k+1
as its
i
th coordinate from the right. In Case 2, we claimed that whenever it is possible to add the
j
th non-diagonal box to the
i
th row of
[\Phi_{v}]
, the weight
v(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})
must have
\overline{j+k}
as its
i
th coordinate, and
k+j+1
as its
(i+j)
th coordinate, counting from the right. Both of these claims will be immediate once we prove the following fact, where
[\Phi_{v}]=(\alpha-1\mid 0)
, so that the row lengths are given by
\alpha_{1}>\cdots>\alpha_{r}>0
:

(5.9)

v(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})=(c_{n},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\alpha_{r}}},\ldots,c_{1},\overline{c_{\alpha_{r}}},\ldots,\overline{c_{\alpha_{1}}}),

where the hats denote missing coordinates.
To prove (
5.9
), first consider the case
r=1
, so that by (
4.3
) we have
v=s_{n,n+1-\alpha_{1}}\cdots s_{n,n}
. We proceed by induction on
\alpha_{1}
. In the base case
\alpha_{1}=1
, we have
v=s_{n,n}
, and so the effect of applying
v
is to negate
c_{1}
. Thus
v(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})=(c_{n},\ldots,c_{2},\overline{c_{1}})
, which agrees with (
5.9
). Now assuming that (
5.9
) holds for
r=1
, let
[\Phi_{w}]
be obtained from
[\Phi_{v}]
by adding the
(\alpha_{1}+1)
th box in the sole row.
Then
w(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})
is obtained from
v(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})
by transposing and negating the
1
st and
(\alpha_{1}+1)
th coordinates from the right; therefore we have

(5.10)

w(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})=s_{n,n-\alpha_{1}}(c_{n},\ldots,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$(\alpha_{1}+1)$th from right}\end{subarray}}{c_{\alpha_{1}+1}},\widehat{c_{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots\ldots,c_{1},\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\uparrow\\
\mathstrut\text{$1$st from right}\end{subarray}}{\overline{c_{\alpha_{1}}}})=(c_{n},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\alpha_{1}+1}},\ldots,c_{1},\overline{c_{\alpha_{1}+1}})

as desired. Proceeding by induction on
r
, we assume (
5.9
) and suppose that
[\Phi_{w}]
is obtained from
[\Phi_{v}]
by adding a row of length
\alpha_{r+1}<\alpha_{r}
. But then
w(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})
is obtained from
v(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})
by applying the result (
5.10
), having replaced
1
with
r+1
, to the string
(c_{\alpha_{r}-1},\ldots,c_{1})
, which thus becomes
(c_{\alpha_{r}-1},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\alpha_{r+1}}},\ldots,c_{1},\overline{c_{\alpha_{r+1}}})
.
This proves (
5.9
), and upon setting
(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})=\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}=(k+n,\ldots,k+1)
, the two claims from Example
5.7
follow immediately.

Types IIIabcd.
The proof is identical in spirit to Type II. The analogue to (
5.9
) is the following: if
[\Phi_{w}]=(\alpha-2\mid 0)
with rank
r
, then we have

w(c_{n},\ldots,c_{1})=(c_{n},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,\widehat{c_{\alpha_{r}}},\ldots,(-1)^{r}c_{1},\overline{c_{\alpha_{r}}},\ldots,\overline{c_{\alpha_{1}}}),

so that there is always an even number of negated coordinates. The only other substantial difference occurs in Types IIIb and IIId, due to the twisting by the
\lambda^{\prime}
from Types IIIa and IIIb, respectively. Hence in Types IIIb and IIId, we must check in the base case (
w={\rm id}
) that
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
are conjugates. Indeed, in both types, when
w={\rm id}
, we have
\pi^{\prime}
a single row and
\pi
a single column of the same length; in Type IIIb this length is
k
, while in Type IIId it is
2k+1
. The rest of the proof imitates Type II, and so the details are left to the reader. ∎

6.
Hilbert series and generalized Littlewood identities

6.1.
Hilbert series of
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}

In this section, we derive the Hilbert series of the infinite-dimensional modules
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}
for the families in Table
2
. Recall from (
5.3
) that
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}\coloneqq L_{\lambda}\otimes F_{-\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta}
.

Let
M
be a highest-weight
\mathfrak{g}
-module with highest weight
\lambda
, and weight space decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{\mu\leq\lambda}M_{\mu}
. Recall the distinguished element
h_{0}\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{k})
from the beginning of Section
3.2
. The
Hilbert series
of
M
is the formal power series

H_{M}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{\mu\leq\lambda}\dim(M_{\mu})t^{-\mu(h_{0})}.

In the setting of this paper, where the coordinates of
\lambda
are either all integers or all half-integers, each of our Hilbert series is an element of
\mathbb{Z}[[t^{1/2}]]
, and can be written as a rational function of the form

H_{M}(t)=\frac{P(t)}{(1-t)^{d}},

with
P\in\mathbb{Z}[t^{1/2}]
such that
P(1)\neq 0
. Then
P(1)
is the Bernstein degree of
M
, and
d
is the Gelfand–Kirillov (GK) dimension of
M
.

Type

\mathfrak{g}

\lambda

m

d

\nu\in\ldots

P(t)

[2pt]
I

\mathsf{A}_{P+Q-1}

-k\omega_{P}

k

k(p+q+k)

\Par(\min\{p,q\}\times k)

\sum_{\nu}\left(\dim F^{(p)}_{\nu}\dim F^{(q)}_{\nu}\right)t^{|\nu|}

[2pt]
II

\mathsf{D}_{N}

-2k\omega_{N}

2k+1

k(2n+2k+1)

\Par(n\times 2k)
,

even rows

\sum_{\nu}\left(\dim F^{(n)}_{\nu}\right)t^{|\nu|/2}

[2pt]
IIIa

\mathsf{C}_{N}

-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}

k(2n+k-1)/2

\Par(n\times k)
,

even columns

IIIb

\mathsf{C}_{N}

-\frac{k}{2}\omega_{N}+\omega_{k}^{*}

k-1

k(2n+k-1)/2

\Par(n\times k)
,

odd columns

[2pt]
IIIc

\mathsf{D}_{N}

-(2k-1)\omega_{N}

2k

k(2n+2k-1)

\Par(n\times(2k+1))
,

IIId

\mathsf{D}_{N}

-(2k-1)\omega_{N}+\omega^{*}_{2k+1}

2k

k(2n+2k-1)

\Par(n\times(2k+1))
,

odd columns

Table 3.
Data for the Hilbert series
H_{\widetilde{L}}(t)=\frac{P(t)}{(1-t)^{d}}
of
\widetilde{L}=\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}\coloneqq L_{\lambda}\otimes F_{-\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\zeta}
. As in Table
2
, we write
p=P-m
,
q=Q-m
, and
n=N-m
.

Theorem 6.1
.

For each of the six types in Table
2
, let
\widetilde{L}=\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}
as defined in (
5.3
). Then

H_{\widetilde{L}}(t)=\frac{P(t)}{(1-t)^{d}},

where
P(t)
and
d
are given in Table
3
. Furthermore, we have
P(t)=H_{\widetilde{L}^{\prime}}(t)
, where
\widetilde{L}^{\prime}=\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By the transfer theorem in
[
11
]
*p. 623 relating Hilbert series to Enright–Shelton reduction, we have

(6.1)

H_{\widetilde{L}}(t)=\frac{\dim F_{\lambda}}{\dim F_{\lambda^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{H_{\widetilde{L}^{\prime}}(t)}{(1-t)^{d}},

where
d=\dim(\mathfrak{p}^{+})-\dim(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime+})
. Due to the congruence of blocks
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
in Table
2
, we have
\dim F_{\lambda}/\dim F_{\lambda^{\prime}}=1
in (
6.1
). It remains to verify
H_{\widetilde{L}^{\prime}}(t)
and the GK dimension
d
for each type below.

Types I and II.
Note that in these types, we have
\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}=k\zeta^{\prime}
, where
\zeta^{\prime}
is the unique fundamental weight orthogonal to
\Phi(\mathfrak{k}^{\prime})
, as in Definition
5.3
. Recall that the
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
-module
F_{-k\zeta^{\prime}}
is
1
-dimensional. By Theorem 3.1 in
[
10
]
, we have

(6.2)

\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}=L_{k\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes F_{-k\zeta^{\prime}}\cong\bigoplus_{k\geq m_{1}\geq\cdots\geq m_{r}\geq 0}F_{-(m_{1}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}\gamma_{r})}

as a
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
-module, where
\gamma_{1}<\cdots<\gamma_{r}
are Harish-Chandra’s strongly orthogonal noncompact roots for
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, with
\gamma_{1}\in\Pi
. Expanding the
\gamma_{i}
into standard coordinates, we obtain the following well-known specializations (recall that
\lambda^{\prime}
depends on
k
in Table
2
):

(6.3)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{Type I:}&\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\cong\bigoplus_{\nu}(F^{(p)}_{\nu})^{*}\otimes F^{(q)}_{\nu},&\nu\in\Par(\min\{p,q\}\times k).\\
\text{Type II:}&\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\cong\bigoplus_{\nu}(F^{(n)}_{\nu})^{*},&\nu\in\Par(n\times 2k)\text{ with even rows.}\\
\end{array}

Each summand in (
6.2
) contributes
\dim(F_{-(m_{1}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}\gamma_{r})})t^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}}
to
H_{E}(t)
. Since
\nu^{*}=-\sum_{i}m_{i}\gamma_{i}
, it is easy to check that in Type I, we have
|\nu|=\sum_{i}m_{i}
, while in Type II we have
|\nu|=2\sum_{i}m_{i}
. We compute the GK dimension
d
using Proposition
3.1
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Type I:}&d=PQ-pq=(p+k)(q+k)-pq=k(p+q+k).\\
\text{Type II:}&d=\binom{N}{2}-\binom{n+1}{2}=\binom{n+2k+1}{2}-\binom{n+1}{2}=k(2n+2k+1).\end{array}

Types IIIabcd.
Now let
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{n}
. Recall that in Types IIIa and IIIb we have
\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{n}
, while in Types IIIc and IIId we have
\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n}
. By Theorem 22 in
[
11
]
, for
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the following decomposition as a module for
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
:

(6.4)

L_{a\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}+b\omega^{\prime}_{n}}\otimes F_{-(a+b)\zeta^{\prime}}\cong\bigoplus_{\nu}(F^{(n)}_{\nu})^{*},

where
\nu\in\Par(n\times(a+b))
such that exactly
a
columns have odd length. We therefore have the following:

(6.5)

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\text{Type IIIa:}&\lambda^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{n}&\Longrightarrow&a=0,b=k&\Longrightarrow\quad\nu\in\Par(n\times k),\text{ even columns.}\\
\text{Type IIIb:}&\lambda^{\prime}=k\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}&\Longrightarrow&a=k,b=0&\Longrightarrow\quad\nu\in\Par(n\times k),\text{ odd columns.}\\
\text{Type IIIc:}&\lambda^{\prime}=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n}&\Longrightarrow&a=0,b=2k+1&\Longrightarrow\quad\nu\in\Par(n\times(2k+1)),\text{ even columns.}\\
\text{Type IIId:}&\lambda^{\prime}=(2k+1)\omega^{\prime}_{n-1}&\Longrightarrow&a=2k+1,b=0&\Longrightarrow\quad\nu\in\Par(n\times(2k+1)),\text{ odd columns.}\\
\end{array}

(As before, the phrase “even/odd columns” means that all column lengths are even/odd.) Each summand in (
6.4
) contributes
\dim(F^{(n)}_{\nu})t^{|\nu|/2}
to
H_{E}(t)
. We compute the GK dimension
d
using Proposition
3.1
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Types IIIa and IIIb:}&d=\binom{N+1}{2}-\binom{n}{2}=\binom{n+k}{2}-\binom{n}{2}=k(2n+k-1)/2.\\
\text{Types IIIc and IIId:}&d=\binom{N}{2}-\binom{n}{2}=\binom{n+2k}{2}-\binom{n}{2}=k(2n+2k-1).\end{array}

This completes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Hilbert series for modules of invariants and semi-invariants

In Types I–IIIa,
H_{\widetilde{L}}(t)
is the Hilbert series of the determinantal variety containing the matrices in
\M_{p,q}
,
\AM_{n}
, or
\SM_{n}
with rank at most
k
,
2k
, or
k
, respectively. More specifically, the determinantal variety is the associated variety of
L_{\lambda}
, which is called the
k
th Wallach representation
; see
[
15
,
11
,
9
]
. The Hilbert series of these determinantal varieties can also be interpreted combinatorially from a Stanley decomposition of the coordinate ring; this decomposition is obtained from the Björner shelling
[
3
]
of the
k
th order complex on the poset of matrix coordinates, and can be understood entirely in terms of non-intersecting lattice paths and a modified RSK correspondence. Details can be found in
[
29
,
19
]
,
[
4
]
, and
[
6
]
for Types I, II, and IIIa, respectively.

From the perspective of classical invariant theory, let
\mathfrak{g}
be of Type
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{C}
, or
\mathsf{D}
, and let
H
be the complex classical group such that
(H,\mathfrak{g})
is a dual pair (in the sense of Howe duality); hence
H
is
\GL_{k}
,
\Or_{k}
, or
\Sp_{2k}
, respectively. Let
V
be the defining representation of
H
, and let either
W=(V^{*})^{\oplus P}\oplus V^{\oplus Q}
(if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{A}_{P+Q+1}
) or
W=V^{\oplus N}
(if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{C}_{N}
or
\mathsf{D}_{N}
). If
\lambda
is as in Types I–IIIa, then as
\mathfrak{g}
-modules, we have

\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}\cong\mathbb{C}[W]^{H}\coloneqq\{f\in\mathbb{C}[W]\mid f(hw)=f(w)\text{ for all }w\in W,\>h\in H\},

where the right-hand side is called the
algebra of invariants
. Due to the fact (i.e., the first fundamental theorem of classical invariant theory) that the fundamental variants are quadratics, it is customary to write

H_{\mathbb{C}[W]^{H}}(t)=H_{\widetilde{L}}(t^{2}).

As a result, in Types IIIb and IIId, whereas
P(t)
has half-integer exponents,
P(t^{2})
becomes a true polynomial.

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{C}_{N}
and
H=\Or_{k}
, with
\lambda
as in Type IIIb, then as
\mathfrak{g}
-modules we have

\widetilde{L}_{\lambda}\cong\mathbb{C}[W]^{H,\det}\coloneqq\{f\in\mathbb{C}[W]\mid f(hw)=\det h\cdot f(w)\text{ for all }w\in W,\>h\in H\},

where the right-hand side is called the
module of semi-invariants
with respect to the character
\chi=\det
.

Example 6.2
.

In Type IIIb, let
N=3
and
k=1
, so that
H=\Or_{1}=\{\pm 1\}
. The module
\mathbb{C}[W]^{H,\det}
of semi-invariants is the span of the odd-degree monomials in
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]
. In Table
3
, we have
m=0
, so that
n=3
and
d=3
. Since
\nu
ranges over partitions in
\Par(3\times 1)
with columns of odd length, the sum over
\nu
ranges over the two partitions
(1)
and
(1,1,1)
. We observe that
F^{(3)}_{(1)}=\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
F^{(3)}_{(1,1,1)}=\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{3}
, with dimensions
3
and
1
, respectively. Hence from Table
3
, we have the following Hilbert series (after replacing
t
by
t^{2}
):

H_{\mathbb{C}[W]^{H,\det}}(t)=\frac{3t+t^{3}}{(1-t^{2})^{3}.}

This Hilbert series reflects the fact that
\mathbb{C}[W]^{H,\det}
is a free module over
\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2}]
with three generators
x_{1},

x_{2}
,
x_{3}
of degree 1, and one generator
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
of degree 3.

6.3.
Generalized Littlewood identities

Recall from Section
3
that the classical identities (
I
), (
II
), and (
III
) can each be interpreted as the Euler characteristic of the BGG resolution of the trivial representation of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
in Type I, II, and III, respectively. In this subsection, by combining our results in Tables
2
and
3
, we generalize these identities by considering the BGG resolution of the finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-module
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. We first point out one of Littlewood’s identities
[
23
]
*(11.9;5) that has not yet appeared in this paper:

(IIIab)

\prod_{i}(1-x_{i})\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})=\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{(|\pi|+\rk\pi)/2}s_{\pi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),

where the sum is over all self-conjugate partitions
\pi=(\alpha|\alpha)
with
\alpha_{1}<n
. See
[
24
]
*Ex. 5.9 for an interpretation of (
IIIab
) in terms of the root system for Type
\mathsf{B}
.

Theorem 6.3
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the three identities in Table
4
. Moreover,

•

upon setting
k=0
, our identities reduce to the classical identities (
I
), (
II
), and (
III
);

•

upon setting
k=1
, the sum of our identities in Types IIIa and IIIb reduces to the Littlewood identity (
IIIab
).

Type

Identity

\nu\in\ldots

\pi

\ell(\pi)

[2pt]
I

\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\prod_{i,j}(1-x_{i}y_{j})&\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu}(\mathbf{y})\\
=&\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{\ell(\pi)}s_{\pi_{x}}(\mathbf{x})s_{\pi_{y}}(\mathbf{y})\end{aligned}

\Par(\min\{p,q\}\times k)

(\alpha|\beta)\in\Par(p\times q)
;

\pi_{x}=(\alpha+k\mid\beta)
,

\pi_{y}=(\beta+k\mid\alpha)

|\pi|-k\rk\pi

II

\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\prod_{i\leq j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})&\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\\
=&\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{\ell(\pi)}s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x})\end{aligned}

\Par(n\times 2k)
,

even rows

(\alpha+2k+1\mid\alpha)
,

\alpha_{1}<n

\dfrac{|\pi|-2k\rk\pi}{2}

IIIa

\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})&\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\\
=&\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{\ell(\pi)}s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x})\end{aligned}

\Par(n\times k)
,

even columns

(\alpha+k-1\mid\alpha)
,

even rank,

\alpha_{1}<n

\dfrac{|\pi|-k\rk\pi}{2}

IIIb

\Par(n\times k)
,

odd columns

(\alpha+k-1\mid\alpha)
,

odd rank,

\alpha_{1}<n

Table 4.
Identities generalizing the classical identities (
I
)–(
III
). The shorthand
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
is as in Table
1
.

A word on notation: the identities in Table
4
can be stated without any reference to congruent blocks or Enright–Shelton reduction, and so we have omitted the prime symbol on the partitions
\pi
. We therefore continue this convention in the proof below, despite using the fact that these partitions are elements of the poset
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
from earlier in the paper. Likewise, we omit the prime symbol on the weights
\mu
, which earlier we denoted by
\mu^{\prime}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

On one hand, by using (
6.3
) and (
6.5
), we can easily write down the character of
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
as the sum of Schur polynomials ranging over certain partitions
\nu
; this is the sum on the left-hand side of the identities in Table
4
.

On the other hand,
\ch\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
must equal the alternating sum of the characters of the parabolic Verma modules occurring in the BGG resolution for
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. Since tensoring with a
1
-dimensional
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
-module is an exact functor, we can obtain the BGG resolution of
\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
from that of
L_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, by replacing each
N_{\mu}
with
\widetilde{N}_{\mu}\coloneqq N_{\mu}\otimes F_{-\langle\lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\vee}\rangle\zeta^{\prime}}
. Then

(6.6)

\ch\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})}(-1)^{\ell(\mu)}\ch\widetilde{N}_{\mu},

where
\ell(\mu)
is defined to be
\ell(w)
for the unique
w\in\prescript{\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\!}{}{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\mu=w\cdot\lambda^{\prime}
. As noted above, the set
\{\widetilde{N}_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})\}
of Verma modules occurring in (
6.6
) equals the set
\{N_{\pi^{*}}\mid\pi^{*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})\}
, which we understand completely from Table
2
. Hence we can rewrite (
6.6
) as

(6.7)

\ch\widetilde{L}_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\sum_{\pi^{*}\in\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})}(-1)^{\ell(\pi)}\ch N_{\pi^{*}}

where
\ell(\pi)
is defined to be
\ell(\mu)
such that
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
is the preimage of
\pi^{*}
.
Thus, by (
4.9
), computing
\ell(\pi)
is just a matter of recovering
|[\Phi_{w}]|
from
\rows\,\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, which we compute in Table
4
. Hence the right-hand side of each identity in the table is given by (
6.7
), except that we multiply both sides by
\ch S(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
, found in Table
1
.

Setting
k=0
causes the sum
\sum_{\nu}
to become empty. For Types I and II, we then immediately recover the classical identities (
I
) and (
II
). In Types IIIa and IIIb, the
k=0
case is degenerate because the
\pi
’s are simply the stacked diagrams
\llbracket\Phi_{w}\rrbracket
, as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
; hence the condition on the parity of the rank of
\pi
disappears, and the two identities collapse into the Littlewood identity (
III
).

Setting
k=1
in Types IIIa and IIIb, we see that
\nu
must be a single column and so
s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})
is an elementary symmetric polynomial; therefore
\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i}(1+x_{i})
. Adding these two identities together, we obtain

(6.8)

\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})\prod_{i}(1+x_{i})=\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{(|\pi|-k\rk\pi)/2}s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x}),

where (since
m=k-1=0
) the sum ranges over self-conjugate partitions
\pi
, just as in (
IIIab
). Now let
-\mathbf{x}\coloneqq(-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{n})
. Since
s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x})
is homogeneous of degree
|\pi|
, we have
s_{\pi}(-\mathbf{x})=(-1)^{|\pi|}s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x})
. Moreover, since
\frac{|\pi|+\rk\pi}{2}+\frac{|\pi|-\rk\pi}{2}=|\pi|
, the two addends have the same parity if and only if
|\pi|
is even, and therefore

(-1)^{(|\pi|-\rk\pi)/2}s_{\pi}(-\mathbf{x})=(-1)^{(|\pi|+\rk\pi)/2}s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x}).

Thus, if we make the substitutions
x_{i}\mapsto-x_{i}
in (
6.8
), we recover the Littlewood identity (
IIIab
).
∎

Remark 6.4
.

The analogous identities corresponding to Types IIIc and IIId reduce to those in Types IIIa and IIIb, upon replacing
2k
by
k-1
. We can, however, view the identities for Types IIIa and IIIb as the two extreme special cases of a more general identity, since they follow from the first two special cases (
6.5
) of the general branching rule (
6.4
). Specifically, for
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(6.9)

\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{i}x_{j})\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\pi}(-1)^{\ell(\pi)}s_{\pi}(\mathbf{x}),

where
\nu\in\Par(n\times(a+b))
has
a
odd columns and
b
even columns, and where the right-hand sum ranges over all partitions of the form

\pi=\begin{cases}(\alpha+a+b-1\mid\alpha)\text{ with odd rank},&a=0,\\
(\alpha+a+b-1\mid\alpha)\text{ with even rank},&b=0,\\
(\alpha+a+b-1,a-1\mid\alpha,0)\text{ with odd rank}&\\
\qquad\text{or}&\\
(\alpha+a+b-1,b-1\mid\alpha,0)\text{ with even rank},&a,b>0,\end{cases}

for
\alpha_{1}<n
. (When
a=b=0
, the first two cases collapse into the single case in the Littlewood identity (
III
), as mentioned above.) The Frobenius symbols in the last case describe an additional arm, whose length is either
a-1
or
b-1
. In (
6.9
) we also have the more complicated formula

\ell(\pi)=\frac{|\pi|-2(a+b)\lfloor\frac{\rk\pi}{2}\rfloor-(-1)^{\rk\pi}a}{2}.

7.
Open problems

Problem 1
.

Find a bijective proof for Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
.

Let
\SSYT(\lambda,n)
denote the set of all semistandard Young tableaux with shape
\lambda
and maximum entry
n
. A bijective proof of Theorem
2.2
would exhibit a bijection

(7.1)

\SSYT(\pi,n)\longleftrightarrow\SSYT(\pi^{\prime},n+1)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for each
\pi=(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)
with
\alpha_{1}<n
. As a first step toward a solution, we can exhibit a bijection

(7.2)

\bigcup_{\pi}\>\SSYT(\pi,n)\longleftrightarrow\bigcup_{\pi}\>\SSYT(\pi^{\prime},n+1),

where both sums range over partitions
\pi=(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)
with
\alpha_{1}<n
. Specifically, we have bijections

(7.3)

\bigcup_{\pi}\>\SSYT(\pi,n)\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{n}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n+1}\longleftrightarrow\bigcup_{\pi}\>\SSYT(\pi^{\prime},n+1),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\text{graphs on $n$ labeled vertices \emph{with} loops but \emph{without} multiple edges}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\{\text{graphs on $n+1$ labeled vertices \emph{without} loops and \emph{without} multiple edges}\}.

The easiest of the three bijections in (
7.3
) is
\mathcal{G}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n+1}
: to each graph in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
, we add a new vertex
v_{n+1}
, and then resolve each loop
(v_{i},v_{i})
by replacing it with an edge
(v_{i},v_{n+1})
. The inverse begins with a graph in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n+1}
, deletes the vertex
v_{n+1}
, and then replaces each edge
(v_{i},v_{n+1})
with the loop
(v_{i},v_{i})
.

The other two bijections in (
7.3
) can be obtained using variations on Schensted row-insertion, both detailed by Burge
[
5
]
.
(Burge’s method actually yields combinatorial proofs of the Littlewood identities (
II
), (
III
), and (
IIIab
), along with their reciprocals.) Similarly to Knuth
[
17
]
, Burge defines an algorithm he calls INSERT 4 (and an inverse algorithm DELETE 4) that gives a bijection
\bigcup_{\pi}\SSYT(\pi,n)\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{n}
, where
\pi
is of the form
(\alpha+1\mid\alpha)
.
Burge defines another algorithm INSERT 3 (and its inverse DELETE 3) that gives a bijection
\bigcup_{\pi}\SSYT(\pi^{\prime},n+1)\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{n+1}
, with
\pi
as before. Upon composing all three bijections in (
7.3
), we therefore have the bijection (
7.2
).

Example 7.1
.

Let
n=3
, and
\pi=(2|1)
. Then the bijection (
7.2
) restricted to
\SSYT(\pi,3)

As long as the bijection (
7.2
) preserves the conjugate shapes of individual tableaux, we would have the desired bijection (
7.1
). Unfortunately this is not the case. In general, the bijection (
7.2
) does
not
restrict to a bijection (
7.1
) for each individual shape
\pi
, as evidenced by the following pairing:

We have not yet found a way to modify the bijections in (
7.3
) in order to yield a bijection (
7.2
) that preserves conjugate shapes.

Problem 2
.

\dim F^{(s)}_{\sigma}=\dim F^{(t)}_{\tau}
.

\dim=6

Figure 5.
A sporadic example of congruent blocks, where corresponding poset elements are not given by conjugate partitions. On the left we have
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
, for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{6}
with
\lambda=(\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{2})
. On the right we have
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
, for
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{4}
with
\lambda^{\prime}=(1,1,0,0)
.

Theorem
2.2
constitutes a first step toward the solution of this problem, in giving the infinite families where
\sigma=(\alpha+m\mid\alpha)
,
\tau=\sigma^{\prime}
, and
t=s+m
. This problem is similar in flavor to the question of classifying the equalities
\binom{a}{b}=\binom{c}{d}
of binomial coefficients. There is an infinite family of these discovered by Lind
[
21
]
and Singmaster
[
27
]
; moreover, de Weger
[
7
]
has conjectured that this family comprises
all
nontrivial equalities, with the exception of seven sporadic instances.

Closely related is the problem of classifying all instances of congruent blocks (in the context of Hermitian symmetric pairs). Outside the families in Table
2
, there exists at least one sporadic example of congruent blocks
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{D}_{6}
with
\lambda=-2\omega_{6}+\omega^{*}_{1}=(\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{1},\overline{2})
. Applying Enright–Shelton reduction, we obtain
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}_{4}
with
\lambda^{\prime}=(1,1,0,0)
. Upon twisting the weights in
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
and
\Lambda(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
by subtracting
-2\omega_{6}
and
2\omega_{4}
, respectively, we obtain the posets
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})
and
\tL(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}})
shown in Figure
5
. We label each poset element with the dimension of the
\mathfrak{k}
-module (or
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
-module) with the corresponding highest weight. Note that the blocks
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
are indeed congruent, although their corresponding poset elements are not given by conjugate partitions.
